COUNTY OF CHESTER. REGULATIONS OF MARKETS AND FAIRS AND THE CLEANSING AND DISINFECI lON THEREOF. NOTICE IS HEREBY 3IVEN, that the following is a copy of an ORDER issued by the Board of Agriculture : REGINALD PO rTs, Clerk to the Cheshire County C)uncil. Chester, 28th July, 1903. (6714). ORDER OF THE BOARD OF AGRICULTURE. (Dated 23rd June, 1903.) MARKET.S AND SALES ORDER OF 1903. The Board et Agriculture, by virtue and in exercise of the powers in them veered under the Diseases of Animals Acts, 1894 and 1896, and of every other power enabling them in this behalf, do order, and it is hereby ordered, as follows: REGULATION OF MARKETS AND SALES. 1.—(1.) A mai ket or sale ot animals shall not be held In or upon any market-place, highway, saleyard or otter premises, until after the expiration of eight days from the date on which the premiers were previously FO used, uulrs the premises, or • part thereof sufficient to accommodate:the number of animals usually exposed at such market or sale, are so paved with cement.concrete, asphalt, or otter bard material impervious to water, as to permit of the same being effectually cleaneed by washing. (2.) This Article shall not be deemed to pr3hibit the bolding of any lawful fair en two or more consecutive days. CLEANSING AND DISINFECTION OF MARKETS AND SALES 2.—(1.) Any market-placs, highway, saleyara or other promises. in or upon which markets or sales are habitually held, shall, as soon as practicable, after beibg used for the purposes of a ma ket or site, and before being again so used, be chewed and disinfected as fo lows : (I) The premises shall be thoroughly scraped or ' swept,anu such parts thereof as permit of the same, being eff .ctually cleansed by washing shall be so cleansed: (ii.) After such cleansing the premises shall be thoroughly sprinkei with a solution el carbolic acid and licnewanh containing not less than five per cent: of actual carbolic or cresylic acid. (iii.) All pens, hurdles, and fi tinga used in connec- tion with the market or sale shall, as soon as prac- ticable, atter being used for such purpose, and before being again so used, be cleansed by scraping and washing, and after such cleansing shall be tt.orougbly sprinkled with a solutim of carbolic acid and Ihnewaah se prescribed above ; and (iv.) The scrapings and sweepings shall forthwith be well mixed with quicklime and be fffectually removed from contact with animals. (2.) The cleansing and disiofection required by this Article ehall, in the case of a market-place saleyard, or other premises in the occupation of any person, los carried out by that person, and in any ,rther case be carried out by the Lecsl Authority of the District. LOCAL AUTHORItt TO ENFORCE ORDER. 3. The Fravisions of this Order, except her it is otherwise provided. 5141 be executed and enforced by the Local Authcrity, PFERCES. 4.—(1.) If a market or sale of animals is held in coo- travention of this Order, the person holding the same, and the owner or consignee of (ash animal exposed thereat, and the person exposing the same thereat, and the purchaser thereat of any animal.such last-mentioned person or such purchaser knowing the market or sale to be held in contravention as aforesaid, shall, each according to an i in respect of his own acts and defaults, be deemed guilty of an offence against the Act of 1894, (2.) If anything is omitted to be (1018 as te2arcis cleansing or disinfection in contravention of this Orthr, the Owner and the lessee and the coupler of any place in or in respect of which the same is omitted shall, etch according to and in respect of his own acts and defaults be deemed guilty of an rffence against the Act of 1894. REVOCATION OF REGULATIONS OF L,CAL AUTHORITIES. 5.—A1l regulations made by a Lnc,l Authority under any Order of the Board requiring hit cleansing and dis- infection of any premises are as from the commence- ment of this 0 der h reby revoked s 9 far as they extend to the cleansing and d'sinfection of any market-place, highways, salward, or ether premises which by this Order are required to te cleansed and disinfected. FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

There is little that is actually new in thr world of dress, and one has to be content tc ring the changes on the many charming and fascinating modes which were launched earlier in the season. Simplicity of style is, however, what all should aim at, particularly for washing dresses, although the embriodery used for trimming purposes may be as rich as one's means and inclinations permit, and when this happy combination is arrived at the result is at once recognised as the height of elegance. For summer wear the simple cotton frock ranks high in popularity, and, as it must frequently visit the laundry and return none

===== 0002601_19030801_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
oTTON GOWN

the worse for its sojourn thereat, both skirts and bodices are plainly made with as little trimming as possible. Tuckings of the material itself, or of embroidery arranged in graduated lines, make the prettiest trimming for washing skirts. Nothing really looks cleaner or prettier than a white ground spotted in black, pale blue, green, or red, trimmed with lace of a coarse make, or em- broidery, the fulness of the blouse bodice being caught in at the waist by a nicely swathed ribbon band with knotted ends at the back. Indeed, it is in the smaller de- tails of dress, such as belts, cravats, or sleeves that originality is displayed. The smaft dress sketched is of white spotted with black, with a belt of apple-green silk finished at the back with two small ends.,

It is really curious to note how prevalent is the swathed silk belt on all the smartest frocks. A short time ago nothing was on view in the shops but a ghaped narrow band, but these latter are only seen now expressed in patent leather or suede, and look very smart worn in conj unction with linen or serge frocks. Belts of patent leather have a par- ticular vogue, and assuredly there is no fitter completion to the simple linen dress. A cream crash gown can be worn with any of the

A PRETTY HATJ

brightly-coloured belts, provided the waist they are destined to encircle is not of large proportions, and that the note of colour is further emphasised in the hat or sunshade, or, perchance, an occasionally revealed dainty under-skirt The ribbon or swathed silk belt is the newest idea, but requires careful fitting, and must be skilfully cut, or it will not prove a success.

To some people the constant repetition of the linen frock is wearisome, and, as a relief, come the delicately shot taffetas gowns, which are not the newest fashion, but, while pretty and chic, are not so expensive as many of the daintiest muslin frocks. These sowns are generally made in a picturesque styl, with full skirts and swathed bodices, the latter draped with a lace or muslin fichu round the shoulders. The prettiest taffetas are self-shot in such old-fashioned colours as vieux rose, lavender, or green. The pretty hat gt etched is principally made of creamy lace, and has little or no crown: The brim is curved up slightly at on side, and blush and crimson roses, with their

foliage and buds, cover the bandeau at the left side, and rest on the hair. A hat of this description might be termed a glorified cape- line. A dainty hat, of course, demands a fine day, and more useful, though less orna- mental, than the lace hat is the new plateau French sailor-hat in satin straw with a ruche of ribbom or a wreath of flowers obscuring and pretensions to crown of which it is possessed. Flowers closely massed together fill in the space between the hat, and the hair at the back for these hats are worn tilted over the face in front. Very smart and business-like are the hats of stitch - coloured ribbon trimmed by a twist of foulard or very soft, satiny ribbon knotted into a careless bow at one side or the back, with ends falling on the hair. One of the prettiest trimmings for the flat sailor-hat is a wreath of roses shading from darkest crirmon to a pale, lovely pink. The little linen coatee sketched is one which I can confidently recommend for seaside or country wear. It is of cerise coloured linen lined with white, and looks exceedingly smart with either a dark or light skirt. • It is double- breasted, and fastens wide dull gold buttons. An ivory cloth coat of the same cut, trimmed with bands of crimson, black and green em- broidery, looks very distinguished, tvhile even newer are the little shot or chine taffeta coats with a deep cape collar of filet lace or embroidery, edged round with ruching,. of 311 k or ribbon tied in a loose bow in front with the ends finished with silk fringe. There are also the long coats of self- coloured linen, made like the Guards' coats, 'which, in navy blue, were so popular in the tarlier part of the season. These are, of zourse, belted in at the back, and fasten down lie front with We buttons. They look xpially well for driving or for seaside or JOuntry wears

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The enthusiasm of the welcome extended to the King and Queen by the inhabitants of Dublin and Belfast, , has been fully equal to the expectations that had been formed, and indeed it is impossible, even among so 1 warm-hearted a people as the Irish, to imagine a more i cordial greeting than that which their Majesties received. The King and Queen manifested in a very marked way that happy attitude which they possess of adapting themselves to every environment, and of inter- esting themselves in everything that is of importance to their subjects. Their solicitude for the poorest dwellers in the city of Dublin, was particularly notice- able, and the people did not fail to shew their apprecia- tion of their Majesties' kindly sympathy. The Prince and Princess• of Wales have paid many country house visits this summer, and the Prince will shortly be the guest of the Duke of Richniond and Gordon for some salmon fishing and shooting at Gordon Castle. The Venerable Duke was not at Goodwood for the races, but left early for Scotland, which will now become the 'principal rendezvous for Society. Many house parties have been made up, and it is expected that Deeside will be specially favoured this year with the presence of Royalty. The alterations and new accom- modation added to Balmoral are taken to indicate that , the King intends to entertain there on a more extensive 1 scale this autumn. _ _ _ _ _

The Duchess of Newcastle is one of the many women of title who prefer country life to the tinsel joys of a London season. She has never had any desire to attain the position of a society leader, but she knows a great deal about dogs, and, as a judge and exhibitor, she is always a prominent figure at the principal dog shows. Though married in 1889 when she was Miss Kathleen Candy, the Duchess is still quite young, being only 32. She is ardently devoted to field sports of all kinds,being a good angler, and an excellent shot, and she has travelled with the3Duke in North America, and has shot big game in the rocky mountains. It is easy to sympathise with the hostess who com- plains in the Press of the worry and fatigue involved in entertaining. Unless one is in the fortunate position of being able to keep a full and competent staff of , servants, the entertaining of a small country house party [ often means nothing lets than slavery for the time being for the hostess. It is upon her that the exacting I ordeal falls of seeing that everything goes smoothly land successfully, not only in the house, but also with regard to the amusements and diversions of her purely pleasure seeking guests. Thelady who complains would like her husband to take a larger share of these duties, but does not see how it is to be done. It is not easy to help her with suggestions, but though she cannot get rid of her responsibilities as hostess, she can exercise her choice of guests, and whilst there are people whom it is always easy to entertain, there are others for whom one mi my labour n vainto please or amuse. The Stage Society discussed at its annual meeting the old question of ladies' hats in theatres, and adopted a very drastic resolution on this subject. It decided "that all ladies among the audience must remove their hats or bonnets during the society's performances, and that attendants at the theatre be instructed to enforce this rule in every possible way." If the attendant were to literally carry out such instructions he would he justified in removing a lady's hat by force, if this were necessary, but he would be a very bold attendant who essayed such a task. Speaking generally, the proprietors of theatres are themselves much to blame for the reluctance of many women to part with their headgear, owing to excessive charges,and the cramped accommodation provided in the cloak rooms. One can never be sure that the hat left at these places will not spend its time on the floor or be otherwise maltreated, but if a large room was pro- vided with lockers which could be opened on the penny- in-the-slot system, and closed with a key, most women would be willing to part with their hats when entering a place of amusement. Such a system would give them ease of mind, and avoid the crush and delay that invari- ably attends the recovery of one's possessions at the cloak room, when the performance is over. In these days when so many articles of clothing can be bought ready made, it is a distinct advantage to be what the shopkeepers call stock size." Many women who go bargain hunting at the sales find their purchases require so much altering and fitting that they scarcely pay for the time and trouble they take up. Unless the homeworker is a very good neealewoman this process is liable.to end in failure, and even if one has a clever maid to help, it is well to be careful in purchasing ready made articles that cannot easily be altered. Those women who number among their recent " bargains " lengths of ribbon,can very easily find uses for them. For example plain, soft taffetas ribbons fold into the high draped belts now so modish; printed or painted mousseline ribbons make dainty sashes for muslin, lace, or tulle gowns, or run up with insertion lace, make delicate waistcoats for wear under a bolero, or open-fronted coat. Then a plain skirt may easily be brought up-to-date by the addition of three or four rows of quilted ribbon, graduating uplvards from the hem to the knees, at widening intervals. For millinery purposes, short lengths of ribbons are always useful,and indeed there would seem to be no limit to the ways by which a woman can successfully dispose of the safe and desirable investments.

Blue serge is the fabric for knock-about gowns, and these are being built to quite clear the grcund. This old familiar fabric is capable of being superlatively smart when well chosen, and well cut, but there are points to remember about it. In the first place it must not be too dark to look like blue—giving one the impression of rusty black—or aggressively blue, which is not only unbecoming, but not suitable for bard wear, or the British climate. The real navy blue is the tone to be secured,and the cut and make should be both plain and business like. The delights of the short skirt are too many to enumerate, but among them may be men- tioned the boon of being able to carry an umbrella sanely, without endangering the eyes of fellow beings by tucking it under the arm, or against the shoulder, and the fact that under such happy circumstances parcels are no longer an impossibility. A pretty sweet may he made as follows :—Boil some wgar till it takes a light golden brown colour, allow- ing half a pint of water to a pound of sugar. Line some little moulds with rataflas, dipping the sides in the sugar to make them stick together. When cold remove the moulds, gently fill the baskets just before serving with whipped cream, ,sweetened and flavoured to taste.

VAINLY ENDEAVOURED TO FIND

HEALTH.

I Many people spend years in:a:vain search for good health. Remedy after remedy is tried. -Doctors and hospitals are consulted, but still the good fortune of perfect health is never theirs. One of our representatives re- cently bad a talk with a lady . whose life has been one long battle with sickness. 'lndigestion and stomach troubles had been the bane of my life," she said. 'A burning pain would seize me in the stomach and bowels. This, accompanied with a feeling of fulness and tight- ness, would cause me to feel insufferably hdt. Worn, listless and tired, I long sought for some remedy that should restore me to my former health. All in vain, I tried first one patent medicine and then .another; they were all alike, useless. My misery and pains were still my daily companions. Sometimes I could hardly breathe, and the hot, feverish, tight feel- ing almost seemed to be pressing the life out of me. I tried doctors at Normanton, but I might as well have kept my money in my pocket, for my health after their treatment was as bad as ever. I read of Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills, and, with some doubts as to their virtues, I bought a bottle. It is the best purchase I ever made in my life, for they have entirely freed my system from the many pains that hourly attacked it, and I am now once more enjoying a better state of health than I have for a long time experienced." Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills are a positive cure for liver and kidney complaints, biliousness, indigestion, constipation, piles, dyspepsia, foul breath, sallow complexion, and female ailments. They are a perfect blood purifier, curing eczema,pimples and blotches. Sold by leading Chemists and Stores, price Is. lid. per bottle. Sir H. M. Stanley and the Schoolboy.

The annual distribution .of prizes, awards, and certificates at Kenyon Hall College took place On Saturday afternoon under the chairmanship of Captain. T. E. Withington, J.P., of Culcheth Ball, who dwelt upon the importance of physical training, and complimented the pupils on their skill in this particu- lar. In the course of the proceedings. the Principal read the following letter from Lady Stanley :—Furze Hill, Purbright, Surrey, July 24th. 1903. Dear Mr. Granville,—my "dear husband—Sir H. M: Stanley— though out of danger, is still quite an. invalid, so he asks me to write to you for him. Hers much tOuched by your letter, so full of kind appreciation, and he is Much interested to hear of Master J. Clowes Sutton's success, and the promise that success holds out for the future. He says England wants many boys like that—earnest thoughtful'youths, who do what they undertake thoroughly, with all their heart. My dear husband always loved those lines :_ ,

"'Joy's soul lies in the doing And the rapture of pursuing • Is the prize." • Will you give Master Clowes Sutton, Stanley's warm congratulations and earnest wishes for his success and happiness through life, and with very kind greet- ings to yourself, Believe us, very truly yours, Dorothy Starley,and Henry M. Stanley."—Master Sutton,as our readers will probably guess, is the grandson of Mr. J. C. Sutton, clerk to the WalTington Board Of Guardians.

The ,turday, August 1 -r 3 1903

p 4 Eazz

had flashed across his mind as he hurriedly cast about for some place of refuge. The miners had struck a tiny spring in their efforts to find gold, and at the bottom of the adit was a mirti.4-. stream of trickling water. It would be useful lit case of a siege.

He passed over the brow of two more hills, and descended a small valley to the beach. Then he returned towards Garth till he- leachcd the niot,th of .the adit. It looked black r•:nd dismal in the moonlight. The ascent was a difficult one for a man la,den with half a hundredweight of food, but he accomplished .it in safety. As he craivlcd in at the narrow entrance, the cold chill of Water struck through the knees of his trousers, and he could hear it dripping from the roof. It was a miserable place for even a wild beast to shelter in.

When he had gone a few yards down the tunnel, he struck a . match. The walls were green with slime, and they glis- tened in the light. He moved on rapidly, striking one match after another till he reached the end of the tunnel. Here there was a cross-cut, running twenty yards each way into the walls. It had been bored a foot above the bottom of the adit, and was comparatively free from water. • He put down the sack of provisions in a dry place, and returned to the mouth of the tunnel. The dawn was breaking over the mountains in the east, and the smooth sea glimmered coldly in the twilight. The tide was low, and. at this point a long, narrow stretch of shingle ran out for more than half a mile into the water.. It was the famous Sam of Cefyn.

Tradition has it that in the far past this part of Wales was originally joined to Ireland, and that the Sam is the last iconnecting link between the two countries. For ten miles out it is visible under the water, though fifty yards on either side of it the sea-bed lies, eighty fathoms deep. The fishermen say that in low tides and clear waters they can see the ruins of a city at the far end of. it. And more than one has told tales of strfAnge dim shapes that swayed and moved and Oicieci in the green water, of faint music dianted by ghostly voices, of the clear notes ot a bell ringing slowly in the distant depths. Tredegar had heard all these tales in his boyhood, and he idly recalled them as he looked out across the sea. He even thought that he could hear the voices calling to him across the waters and urging him to fly from England. Then on the distant hills he saw the dark woods round Llynglas, and knew that he could not go, and that he must watch over MaVanwy till this foul creature had been killed or captured, and that he must rick his own life to be near her.

As the sun rose, and the whole panorama of the bay was flooded with light, he retired to the cross-cutting and laid down to rest. For -he had to sleep by day, and go forth at night, when the thing he was in search of was abroad. Then he closed his eyes and lay down in the darkness, and listened to the drip-drip of water from the tunnel roof till he went to sleep. For a whole fortnight Tredegar lived in his burrow. He never stirred from it by day, but every night he would creep stealthily inland and' make his way by a long, cir- cuitous route to the woods round Ilynglas. Here he would watch and listen through the hours of darkness, always on the alert in case Mavanwy needed his assistance, yet never showing himself or attempting to com- municate with her. More than once he saw her dear face, and was so close to her that a whisper would have reached her ears. But he was silent. They • had said the last word —" Good-bye !" And though he suffered agonies from his silence, he had resolved not to speak again till his name was clear from dishonour.

. And through all these weary days he never heard the sound of a human voice, save in the distance; nor could he find any trace of the thing he sought, save only that strarige web in the Tredegar woods. Hour after hour on his journey homeward he would sit among the trees at the foot of the • mound and watch for the creature to appear: But he never saw it, though he knew it was still alive and in the neighbourhood, for fresh animals were fastened to the web each time he saw it. He saw John Walroyd twice again in these nightly vigils. - Once on the lawn of Llyn- glas with Mr. Morgan, and once going through the Iredegar woods with another small bundle. He puzzled hi S brain for any solu- tion to this new mystery. He once more followed Walroyd, and once again saw the boat run into the shore and Mr. Cantrip land from it. But he gathered no information about theit business.

He slept most of the day, but at early dawn .and evening he would sit near the mouth of his tunnel and drink in the sunlight on the shore and sea. He rarely saw anyone pass 'along the beach. An occasional fisherman with a prawn net, poking about in the pools and crevices of .the rocks; an old woman and her little grandson gathering driftwood for the fire ; now and then a small party of summer visitors. But visitors were few in 'Garth that year. The terror of the place had been noised abroad in every newspaper in England, and the landladies waited in vain for the money that would keep them in corn- fort during the winter. _ _ _

Tredegar waited and watched from the side of the cliff like, an eagle perched on a crag. And in the long hours of silence he tried to find some solution of the things he could riot understand. But he tried in vain to pierce the veil. All was darkness.

Then one evening in August a dazzling flash of light broke across the gloom, and left behiad it a horror that was more terrible than all the blackness of the night.

' It was nearly nine o'clock, and Tredegar was just starting on his nightly journey to Llynglas. There was still a faint glow of crimson in the West, but it was dark enough for him to escape observation. The moon was not up, and the whole land was wrapt in grey shadows. A few yellow lights twinkled along the shore by Garth. He slid over the, edge of the entrance, and descended slowly • and silently to the 'beach. When he had reached the bottom he crept along close under the cliff tow4ds the south. It was high tide, and in the ether direction the sea washed .deep against half a- dozen points between him and Garth. * He had nearly reached the little qove where the cliffs ran down to the level of the shore, when suddenly the grey figure of a woman moved out of the shadow of a rock and stood in his path. He stopped, and crouched low to the ground. • She advanced towards him fearlessly. At that time few Women in the neighbourhood would have dared to come out after nightfall, and the appearance of a man wonld have sent them away shrieking with terror. They were all afraid of meeting the madman, Emrys Tredegar. "tut this \S( man -walked up close to him. She was evidently a stranger. It. was he who turned to fly. But before he had gone a couple of yards a voice called his name, and he stopped. " Mavanwy !" he said, in a low voice, " Is that you ?"

The woman did not answer, hut came to his side, and laid her hand upon his arm. A silk scarf muffled her face.

" El-Drys," she repeated; " I want to speak to you."_

• And then he recognised the voice, and knew who it was.

"You here, Cynthia!" he cried, in astonish- ment. " What -brings you here.? At this time—now ?"

"What should bring me here ?" she broke in, passionately. "T have Come to save you ! I have come in time I I have tracked you to Garth. One of the detectives is dead. They say you killed him 1 The news in the papers brought Me clown. For a fortnight I have searched and watched and Waited. , I have run you down at last ! You must leave, here at once ! This very evening a fisherman caught sight of something moving in your hiding-place. They .are going to hunt the beach •to-night. Emrys, you must leave tt once! Here, I have money! Take it, but go at once !" - • And .she pulled out a thick 'wad of bank- notes and thrust it into his hand.

You are generous, Cynthia," he said, handing her back the notes. "But I cannot go." 4,11 1" she cried, in a hard voice. I; that

other woman! Yon cannot leave her I Then stay here and die— Oh, no, Ernrys, forgive me! I only want to save you! Leave this part of the beach before they come here. I implore you I" "Yes," Tredegar replied; I must find another lair for to-morrow. But you—l cannot leave you here! Even men are afraid to go out alone at nights." " I am not afraid of you, Emrys," she Aitit a smile.

He laughed bitterly. " No, no, of course not," he replied. " Wit there; is real danger abroad—something too terrible to contemplate! If it is not caught soon„ I shall probably suffer for its crimes. But I will see you home to Garth. You are staying there, I suppose ?" "Yes, with my father," she answered. He knows Mr. Walroyd, and has business with him. I wanted a rest, and persuaded him to bring rue here. It was only a short time ago that I found out that he knows this

CHAPTER Xi.—Continued.

The crunch of Walroyd's ieet on the shingle beneath had grown fainter and fainter as Tredegar had proceeded Or his way, till at last it died out altogether. But, looking over the edge, he saw Walroyd 'trudging slowly along the beach, a mere speck in the moon- He lay down and watched him. Then he suddenly . started. Some three hundred yards behind Wairoyd another figure moved stealthily from boulder to boulder, and as he watched it he noticed that it moved in a very curious manner, not upright as a man, but on all fours like a beast. And yet not altogether like a beast,. for every now and _ _

then- it would move swiftly forwa-rd on two legs and decrease the distance between itself and John Walroyd. The latter appeared to be entirely unaware of its approach.

- Tredeear's blood ran cold at the sight. He had seen that ungainly movement once before. If John Walroyd did not turn and see his danger, he 'was a dead man. Tredegar picked up a stone and sent it flying on to the rocks below. Walroyd turned round sharply, and the pursuer sank fiat on the ground behind a small rock. And then, for the first time, Tredegar saw that something else moved on the beach nearly a quarter of a mile behind the thing that crouched among the pebbles. After a pause of two or three minutes, Walroyd continued his journey, but the thing still remained motionless by the spur of grey rock, and the newcomer crept nearer and nearer in the shadow of the cliff. Tredegar rose to his feet and ran as hard as he could .vt-tr the brow of the ascent. Three hundred yards further on the cliff toped down again to a height of one hundred and fifty feet, and at this point a narrow winding path led to the beach. He ran as though the devil were at his heels.

In- Jess than five minutes he had reached, the beach, and was moving slowly and softly over some ridges of slate to the projecting corner of the cliff which hid everything from his sight. When he reached it, he saw that the boat was close inshore against a small platform of rock, and that Walrovd was talking to the man in the bows. He looked carefully along the beach to the north, but could see nothing of the other two figures. A high spur of rock ran down almost Po the water's-edge from the place where he was standing. He looked at it enquiringly; the top was' broad and even, and if he could reach it, he could crawl along it to within a few yards of the boat. He tried two or three places, but with no success. He could not afford to risk a fall on the shingle. He knew that Walroyd was armed, and would not hesitate to fire on him. He would be shot like a wild bcast, and the whole country- side Would applaud his murderer. He could not, therefore, attempt any ascent where he could not be certain of a sure foothold.. At last, however, he found such a place, and, reaching the summit, crawled along to the end of the ridge.

He looked Over the edge, and saw that Wal-

rovd was still talking to the _stranger. The latter was standing now on the rock, and his back was towards Tredegar. He was a small man, and his head did not reach to Walroyd's shoulder. Then he suddenly turned, and Tredegar drew in his I;.reath sharply, and could scarcely prevent himself from giving vent to an exclamation of surprise. The little man was Mr. Cantrip. Tredegar puzzled his brain to think of any possible connection between the two men, but in vain. He leaned forward to catch ttieir conversation, but they were speaking in low tones, so that even, the boatmen could not hear them. The whole thing was so unsuspected and incredible that Tredegar could think of no solution to the problem. He had only seen Mr. Cantrip three or four times, and knew nothing about him, but it seemed an inexplicable circumstance that this little old man from River Street should have a midnight appointment with a mil- lionaire in Wales. But he guessed now that there was some mystery ,in the past life of the Walroyds, and he had a vague feeling that he was being drawn against his- will into a strange web of circumstances, and that even the horrible creature that had followed him to England was weaving some of the threads that would ultimately entangle his life. But he could not see into the darkness before him, and now, when a few words might have explained everything, he could only listen and hear nothing but the low murmur of voices. In less than five minutes Cantrip stepped into the boat, and the men pushed out from the shore. Tredegar could see that Walroyd no longer had the bundle at his waist, and that the old man was fingering something lovingly in the bows. The boat made straight out from the shore into the west, and for the first time Tredegar noticed a light twinkling on the horizon, and knew that a ship was waiting in the bay. In less than a quarter of an hour the boat had vanished in the haze upon the sea. But John Walroyd still stood upon the ledge of rock and gazed out across the waters.

Then suddenly a terrible scream broke the silence. There was a sound of pebbles flung up by a man's feet, and a second or two later a figure emerged from the shelter of the cliff —the figure of a man running as though for his life. A few yards behind him came a huge brown form, moving swiftly in leaps and bounds. Tredea°ar rose to his knees as they neared the rock, and looked round for some missile, but the-surface of the ridge was bare as a billiard-table. Walroyd's • hand went sharply to his pocket, and the barrel of his revolver glittered , in the moonlight. He raised it, but dared not fire. The two men were coming for him in a straight line. .

Then The foremost Man fell' on his face with a crash. Quick as thought Walroyd seized the opportunity and fired at the pursuer. He missed and the bullet spattered against the rock behind, and before he could fire again the two forms • rolled over and over on the ground, and there was a sound of snapping bones and a long, terrible wail of pain.

Walroyd was no coward. He knew when he fired that the murderer of three men was bs.fore him.' •He ran, hastily towards. the struggling mass of limbs so as to get in a shot without hurting 'both of the combatants, but fortune was against :him. His 'foot slipped on •a piece of wet seaweed, .and he went crashing to the ground. His revolver flew out of his hand.

He rose to his feei„ but before he could regain the weapon, he • was seized from behind and flung down in a heap among the pebbles. He did not stir. The creature leant over him, and then stooped and caught hini by the arms, .but before it could do anything further, its throat was seized in a powerful grasp, and it was forced slowly backwards from its prey. Tredegar had been watching the • whole proceeding from the top of the cliff, and this was the moment he thought fit to interfere. He had no reason to love John Walroyd, but he had every reason for saying him from this adversary. He had slid down swiftly from the rock, and th.rown himself headlong into the combat.

The creature loosed Walroyd. All its energies were required for this new foe. It seized Tredegar by . the collar, and literally tore. off one side of his clothes in a single sweep of its claws. Coat, waistcoat, and shirt hung in shreds from Tredegar's waist, and his bare side was streaked with long red lines of blood. He winced with pain, and drawing. back his right hand, struck the animal so fearful a blow in the face that the blood spurted from it, and its hair was crim- son in the moonlight. But before he could strike again the creature broke from the grasp

of his left hand, and fled. Perhaps, with dumb, brutal instinct, it had recognid its adversary. Perhaps it realised that it had met its match. At any rate, it fled hcrwling along the shore.. *Tredegar sprang to the revolver, which lay shining on the pebbles, and steadying the weapon with his left arm, fired every barrel at his retreating foe. At the best of times he was not a good shot, and in the darkness and excitement of the moment, every one of his bullets went wide of the mark. He flung the revolver on the beach with an oath, and turned to Walroyd. The latter opened his eyes and stared up at the sky. Then he saw .Treclegar, and quickly reach- ing out across the pebbles for his revolver, he aimed it at the latter's face and pulled the trigger, five times in rapid succession. There was no report, and his hard *zeen face grew pale. He rose to his knees, and swung the weapon back to hurl at the man he thought ,to be his adversary. Tredegar quickly caught nold of his wrist and gripped it so tightly that the revolver fell on the stones with a clang. " That will do, Mr. Walroyd," he said calmly, "I don't know why you should want to shoot me. I have just saved your life. If I had been a minute later you would haw been a dead man. F have not come off scot ...lee myself." Walroyd did not answer, but stared wildly at Tredegar's face. The gigantic figure of the latter looked weird and terrible in the moonlight. ,His side was red with blood and :he shreds of his clothing hung down in -'estoons from his waist. A braver man than John Walroyd might have quailed at such an apparition.

You recognise me, I suppose," Tredegar continued.

"Yes," replied Walroyd, "1 recognise you." His face was white as death. Another man might have prayed for mercy, but Walroyd's quick brain was only searching for some means of escape. He glanced quickly to right and left, then suddenly sprang to his feet, and swerved out of the reach of Tredegar's arm. The latter picked up a heavy stone from the shingle and hurled it at the legs of the flying form with such force and accuracy that Walroyd gave a cry of paid and dropped on to the beach. Tredegar came up and caught hold of his arm. "What's this nonsense," he said sharply: "I have just saved your life, and you are flying from me as if I was trying to take it. I suppose -to-morrow you will itell everyone that you have escaped from the mad Tjedegar. You contemptible fool," and his voice rose with passion. "You will find out before long that you are after the wrong man. Why, I could kill you now, as easily as I could kill a rat. Perhaps if I spare you, you will unde;- stand that I am no murderer. But I will ensure your silence till I have left the neigh- bourhood. You untit:terab',7! idiot, if you only knew what it is you hart to fear,- you would pray every night- on your knees to God and thank Him that Einrys Tredegar was at hand to-night to save you." Furious with anger, Tredegar wrenched some strips off his clothing, and bound Wal- royd hand and foot, so that he could not move a limb. Then he carried him farther up the beach, out of reach of the highest possible tide. Walroyci did not speak a word, but he foamed at the mouth with impotent rage. "Do. you know," Tredegar said, as he set him down on the stones, "that I have given you two lives, to-night. You will repay me by hunting me down to the death. But if ever I meet you again, Mr. John T. Walroyd, I will let you know the sort of man I am to deal with."

With these words he left Walroyd and walked over to where another form lay motionless on the beach. He turned the body over so that the moonlight fell upon the face. It was a stranger, a keen, thin, foxy faced man with red hair. Tredegar searched his pockets for some clue to his identity. Among the half dozen letters addressed to— James Wright, Esq., The Red Lion, Ilanfihangel. he found one in a hand that he recognised. It was brief and to the poinc`... "Dear Sir,

"I enclose you a further io. I hope yuu will soon have news of what you seek.—C.C."

Tredegar crumpled the letter in. his great palm, and rising to his feet looked with con- temptuous pity on the face of the dead man. "A detective," he muttered to himself. "In the pay of Cynthia Cantrip. I wonder if she means me well."

BY THE SARN OF CEFYN.

TREDEGAR ran towards Garth as fast as he could make his way over the rocks and shingle. Then he • climbed the cliffs and doubled back swiftly to the South. As he passed the place where he had left John Wal- royd, he peered over the edge and saw that the latter had not moved. Fifty yards away another form lay motionless. It was the body of the stranger, crumpled into a little heap like a dead insect. Treclegar passed the highest point of tha cliff and descended a long slope to a • tiny bay. Then he crossed this and began to ascend the next ridge of hill. A little way inland a farm- house stood white and bare in he moonlight. He looked at It, stopped a moment doubtfully, and then walked. boldly 111) to the gate. A sheep dog began to bark and rattle his chain. Tredegar flung himself upon the animal and strangled it.. He felt as though he had murdered a friend,: for he was passionately fond of dogs. But it was no time for senti- ment. Whatever stood .in his path that night had to be silent or to die. Five minutes later he had bre.)ken into the house and secured every "piece of food he could lay hands on, together with an old coat and a flannel shirt. He. left nothing that could possibly be eaten, and he emerged with a small sack full of loaves, hams, flour, butter, cheese, etc. He gave one glance at the dead collie, patted him tenderly on the head, and fled up the hill. He had decided on' his hiding place,. Two ,miles south of Garth there was 3 srrtS-A hole

in the face of, the cliff. It was about fifty feet from the ground, and not more than four feet :6 diameter. Twenty years before the date of • this story a mining engineer from London had spent his summer at Garth. In the course of his wanderings along the beach he had icked up a piece of , gold quartz, and his practised. eye had detected a reef on the slaty

iace of the cliff. He returned to bondon, haa -in assay made, and found that it ran about ounces to the ton. On the strength of . . .

is he obtained mining rights, floated a- small :ompany, arid they commenced boring an adit in the wall of rock. They took out sixty tons of quartz, and obtained about 2O ounces of gold. For two or three months Wal*s boomed like Australia in the palmy days of Ballarat. Mines sprang up like Mushrooms, and miners in far off lands started to pack up their goods and book passages to England. Then one fine day the reef disappeared, and the company dug through yards and yards of slate in the hope of finding it again. But never another pennyweight of gold did they take out of the concern, and the only asset of the company when it was wound up. was a tunnel some 200 yards in length. It was for 'this place that Tredegar was making. CHAPTER XIII: IN THE TOILS OF THE HUNTER.

FOR one brief moment Tredegar stood ir- resolute. He was thinking not of himself, but of the woman wh9 had come out to save him, and litho would be compromised for life. He turned, and .grasped her arm roughly. "Scream !" he said, a whisper. "Loud, so they can hear you I Quick !" She was silent 4

Tredegar's ' blood boiled. He -could only remember that a fortnight ago he had saved this man's life. "You fools !" he cried. "You are after the wrong man. Let me pass I"

Some, of them Were armed, .and though they would not dare fire while he was in their midst, they would loose a volley directly he got clear of them. It Would, indeed, be a miracle if one of them did not hit him. He realised to the full that they would kill him rather than let him escape. And so-he dashed into their midst for a last good fight. every. muscle in his frame strung

up for a contest that would leave its mark on more than one of his adversaries.

THE NEW EARL POULETT AND HIS MAGNIFICENT MANSION, HINTON ST. GEORGE,

TEE ROMANTIC EARLDOM.

At last the vexed question as to who is the rightful Earl Poulett has been finally settled by the House of Lords, sitting in solemn session on the subject. Technically every peer has a right to sit upon such a question; but the custom is that a Committee of Privi- leges is formed from the law lords. Seven, including the Lord Chancellor, heard the pleadings in the case, under the chairman- ship of the Earl of Morley, and their decision was unanimously in favour of the " infant " claimant, the son of the third marriage, and against "Viscount Hinton," the ex-organ- grinder. The question before them was purely one of the title, as under the settlement of IS 53 the late earl and his father had cut the entail; the property was, therefore, free to be "left at discretion, Hinton St. George is one of the finest -mansions in the west of England, and under his father's will was left absolutely to the newly-established peer. Seldom, if ever, has there been such a sad and • sordid romance in the peerage, and it is verily a case of the sins of the fathers being visited on the children. In his early days the late Earl Poulett met the daughter of a Landport pilot in Portsmouth and married her out of hand, only to separate from her a few weeks later. Six months after the wedding a son, fami- liarly known to the public as "Viscount Hinton," was born, and the point at issue was that he, though born in wedlock, was the son of someone else, and Captain Gran- ville, with whom the Countess, nee Eliza Newman, had been living in Dublin, was sug- gested. The fact remains that the late peer repudiated parentage, contending that his wife was enceinte to someone else at the time of marriage. Though he &insistently lived apart from her, she bore other children and registered them in his name; and the House of Lords, on the 'famous Townshend prece- dent,. held that, though born duly in wed- lock, her son was not the son of her husband'

THE LARGEST STONE BRIDGE.

In these progressive days great engineering feats follow hard on the heels of each othere and new records are set up almost daily. The latest of these is the new bridge at Luxem- burg, which is the largest masonry bridge in the world and is wonderfully handsome. It is 136 feet in height and its principle arch has a span of 263 feet, which makes its appearance peculiarly effective. The bridge has been thrown across the river •Petnesse, which surrounds the town, flowing through a narrow ravine. Luxemburg, which is the

Else- where it is engirt by a steep valley 200 feet deep, in which nestle the industrial suburbs of Klausen, Pfaffenthal and Grund. BARNARD CASTLE'S NEW MEMBER.

Mr. A. Henderson, the new Labour member for Barnard Castle, is a native of Glasgow, and in his 4oth year. His parents immigrating to Newcastle-on-Tyne in 1875, he was apprenticed as a moulder with Messrs. Robert Stephenson & Co., with whom he remained till 1893: On the completion of his appren- ticeship he joined the Friendly Society of Ironfounders, and was soon installed into an official position, and for some years past has occupied a branch or dis- trict office. Since 1894 he has been the -workmen's secretary of the North - East Coast Concilia- tion Board, and in the year of his appointment as such was one of his society's representatives at the Norwich Trades Con- gress. Mr. Henderson has served upon the City Council of Newcastle-on-Tyne, upon the Durham County Council, and upon the Dar- lington Borough Council, of which he is still a member. He is a magistrate for Newcastle. Mr. Henderson, who is married and has a family, is a vice-president of the Darlington Temperance Society, being an abstainer, and a member for nearly 25 years of the Wesleyan body, has filled nearly every office in the connexion open to a layman, being at the present time actively engaged with the Dar- lington East Road Circuit, where his services as a local preacher are often in demand. He was formerly political agent to Sir J. Pease, and took part in the late contest in Woolwich, 6and subsequently at Preston, where he acted as agent to Mr. Hodge.

DEVONPORT'S TRIBUTE.

Seldom has a tour created such local en- thusiasm as that of the Prince and Princess of Wales to their duchy in the West of Eng- land, and from start to close it was most auspiciously carried out. It was their first visit since their accession to the Cornish title, used only for eight months during their fa- mous Colonial tour, and their new lieges were delighted to welcome them and do them homage. The royal family have always been popular in the West; and during his long tenure of the duchy of Cornwall the King always showed a keen interest in this parti- cular part of the country. Now his mantle has descended on the Prince of Wales, who in this, as in everything else, has proved him- self a worthy son of his father. His speeches

A BUSINESSLIKE NOVELIST.

Mr. Benjamin Leopold Farjeon, who has this week been laid to his last long rest, was born in London, and was educated in private schools. In early life he emigrated to'ku,.- tralia, and after some experience of the gold- fields of Victoria removed to New Zealand, where he assis- ted in starting the "Otago Daily Times," at Dunedin, in 186 r, of which o urnal, the first daily paper pub- lished in the colony, he was part proprietor and also man- ager. But this work, exhaust- ing enough for most men, was not sufficient for him, for while he was thus actively employed in

TUF. LATE M

FARJ EON

journalism he

-also devoted much time and study to the composition of plays and novels. He had determined at quite an early age to devote himself to litera- ture: One of his first published works was " Grif," which was issued in 1870, while he was engaged in New Zealand as a journalist, and was very favourably received. It at- tracted the notice of Dickens, and on Mr. Farjeon receiving an encouraging letter from the novelist he decided to come to England. He accordingly did so, and taking up his residence in the neighbourhood of Charing Cross, has since had a very successful career as a story-teller. Among his later productions have been " Samuel Boyd of Catchpole Square," "The Mesmerists,' "Pride of Race," and "The Mystery of the Royal Mail," this latter only published last year. THE KING AND TENNIS.

The world of tennis is highly gratified at his Majesty consenting. to become patron of the Royal Tennis Club, to which belongs the famous covered tennis court at Hampton Court Palace, where his Majesty, in his younger days, frequently played. The court is the oldest in England, having been built by Henry. VIII., who was also a tennis player. Nearly all the modern covered courts have taken this famous old enclosure as their model. Formerly Hampton Court was the scene of all the. great tennis matches of the

day. Tom White, who -in his day occupied a high place among pliyer's, has been the pro- fessional of the, club for the last twenty-three years. The court is. always open to the public, and has innumerable visitors through- out the year. The Royal Tennis Club, by the way, is cherishing the fond hope that the King will, sometime soon, give their famous court a visit and play upon it as he. did in the. days of his youth..

The Palace is the only one that aspires to elegance, and it is a light and airy structure of great beauty. The Gordon College is only half completed. Its style is very plain and massive, but has handsome arcades, giving shelter from the sun and connecting the class rooms. Almost all the build- ings in Khartoum have-been designed and carried out by the Royal Engineers, who happened to be quartered here at the time. The War Office, Post Office, Courts of Justice, are all far advanced. There is sign of preparation for building on many of the blocks and streets already laid out. The new city, will cover several square miles of streets and open spaces, and abundant room is-left for extension. Two banks are in full Operation—the 'Bank of Egypt and National'Bank. Tee hospitable Sudan Club is excel- lent, and situated inn garden of several acres. There is also a Soldiers' Club, a most efficient centre for the non-coms. The Zoological Gardens are beautiful, only wanting the beasts, which will be soon supplied from the game reserves. The Greek merchants are building fine warehouses, gradually removing their depots from Omdurman. One does not see any British merchants' stores ; but they are generally the late copiers, the Greeks are the pioneers.—From " The New Khartoum." in the Windsor Magazine. HOW A STORAGE BATTERY WORKS. The popular impression is to the effect that a storage battery stores, up electricity, and that the bottled-up fluid is held somehow under pressure, to be released on demand like highly charged mineral water. The prin- ciple of the storage battery is quite different from this, as the entire action is chemical, fundamentally. The storage battery is a simple piece of apparatus, mechanically. In its most familiar form it consists of two lead plates supporting active inaterials,a high oxide of lead' for the positive plate, and metallic lead ix a spongy, finely divided state for the negative. The plates are immersed in dilute sulphuric acid and con- tained in a glass jar or wooden tank lined with lead. Two plates, one positive and the other negative, con- stitute a couple, and a cell may be made up of as many couples connected in parallel as the requirements of discharge necessitate. That is, all the positive plates are connected together separately from the negative plates, which are themselves joined in one aggregation independent of the positives. A battery consists of a considerable number of cells connected usually in series—that is, the positive group of couples in each cell is joined to the negative group of the preceding cell so that the electric current passes through all the cells, seriatim. _

Now, "charging" a storage battery simply means passing a continuous electric current through it from i some outside source of supply, as a railway or lighting generator, and thereby causing certain chemical actions to take place. The eaergy of the current is thus stored up as chemical changes and recombinations, or in other words it is transformed into potential chemical energy. " Discharging" a battery is naturally the reverse of the process just indicated. The battery is connected to a railway, lighting, or general power circuit, and it at once begins to deliver current, generated by the reversed chemical actions which begin to take place as soon as opportunity is given for a current to flow. Back goes the battery towards its origlhal uncharged condition, and the cycle may be repeated over and over again, always with Cie same resulting chemical actions caused by, and then causing, the electric current.—Howard S. Knowlton, in The Engineering Magazine for August. THE UNFORTUNATE "GARDEN OF ENGLAND." Kent might almost be describedas the most unfortun- ate county in England this year. The fruit, on which its people depend so largely for their livelihood, has been practically rained, and the dearth of apples, pears, plums, and cherries is remarkable: The worst of it is that no compensating advantage is likely to be gained in the way of large prices, because the importation of fruit is such a rapidly expanding business. There is a saying among the men of Kent thAt a bad fruit year and bad hop year never come together, but we are afraid that this consoling proverb will prove the rule by the exception this year, since the hop prospects at the present moment are about as bad as can be. Blight, mould, and other diseases threaten to ruin a crop that under no circumstances would be large. Yet hops have ft way of recovering towares the end of the season, and it is just possible that the vrospects may improve. Unfortunately no such reassuring view is possible in regard to the —Country Lift. • I put my arm about her Ere she had time to flee. " ' Tis thus 'tis done, fair doubter— You're all the world—to me !" TRUMAN ROBERTS ANDREWS IN SMART SET. THE KING AND THE MAFEKING GUN. "Deposited by His Mzjesty the King" is the legend inscribed upon the famous Mafeking seige gun now exhibited at the Royal United Service Institution. Rough thoughits workmanship may be, there is most certainly no present which the King could have received more proudly at the hands of an officer, or have set more deservedly before the eyes of his subjects. "The Wolf" (as the gun is called, after a nickname of General Baden-Powell) constitutes, indeed, in itself a memorial of British grit and resource and competence which should claim honour, and even reverence, as long as its metal endures. Every part of it bears witness to invention born of desperate necessity. Cast as it was during the seige in an old tank to which the necessary draught WM supplied by the vacuum brake off a rail- way carriage, it was mounted on the wheels of a thresh- ing machine. In truth, there is no en.l to the rough- and-ready expedients which had to be called into play in its manufacture. Nevertheless, here it is, a practic- able gun—a miracle of terrible usefulness, more inspir- ing to the visitor than the mightiest ordnance ever turned out with all the appurtenances and means of a great arsenal.—The King and his Nary and Army. A CREMATION CEREMONY. ' The subject of the disposal of the dead is little under- stood by the majority; it is also one into which senti- ment of the tenderest and most hallowed kind largely enters. Let it, therefore, at once be said that there is nothing in the nature of cremation, as such to wound the most sensitive or repel the most devout. The Writer was enabled to study the process in all its details at the new crematorium opened recently among the country fields at Golder's Green, a few miles out of London. The coffin, covered in purple cloth, and bearing a mass of beaetiful flowers, lay on a narrow catafalque ex- tending at right angles to the wall, in which just at the coffin's foot, were a small pair of metal folding doors. THE SUICIDE OF A WARRIIAIGTON

Inca.

, The Rev. J. Yonge and Uncharitable

Judgments.

"Who are thou that judgest another ?" (James 4, part of v. 12) was the text upon which the Rev. John Yonge based his discourse on Sunday evening in Wycliffe Con- gregational Church. It seemed to him, he said,that the whole social life of our 'town needed rescuing and purifying from a plague of uncharity. People every- where seemed to be affected by a very unhappy epidemic lof uncharitableness, and to have contracted a habit of judging and condemning others whichwas very grievous and distressing. The last few days had witnessed a sad exhibition of it, which, to himself, at least, was exceed- ingly painful. The town, on Monday last was suddenly shocked by a terrible tragedy, the like of which in most ; respects had not occurred in our midst before. The shock had not been merely that suicide had been com- mitted because unhappily, deeds of that kind were not , infrequent. But the shock to the public mind had I come from the fact that the suicide was that of a Chris- tian minister, a preacher of the gospel of salvation, and ; hope and comfort ; a professed believer in God,who is " I the." refuge and strength of them who are in trouble. 1 The effect upon the mind of so sad a tragedy was indeed overwhelming and surely should be productive of the truest sorrow and deepest sympathy. So at lea-st,one might have thought. To his (the preacher's) surprise and dis- appointment, however, he had heard even from many , quarters—very hard judgments of him who then ended his life by his own act. If what had come to his own ; knowledge was typical of a general feeling, there would seem to be far more censure and condemnation of the Vicar's deed than there were pity, compassiwasympathy , for the unbearable grief which impelled him to it! He 1 (the preacher) had been intensely saddened by what appeared to him to be a great failure of Christian charity in this distressing and dreadful case. ‘Vhat was 1 the case ? It was that of a man devoted to his wife with a passionate love such as was not, perhaps very often seen. He came here to live and work because ad- vised that the change of residence might be good for her. She was in a delicate state which gave him hourly solicitude, and to which, night and day, he personally ministered with intensest devotion. He believed her state to have been produced by her faithful, personal watching over him, some few years back, when his own life was in peril for months. Nothing he could do for her was in his idea too much. Last Sunday—all his hopes and labours were defeated—and his wife suc- cumed in death. The blow was greater than he could bear! His heart was overwhelmed! His grief was greater than his faith.. His brain reeled, and he ended his life by his own act. He confessed that, if these were the facts no case could make stronger appeal for compassionate, consideration—or should silence more completely any harsh condemnation. ,Many people had said that the Vicar could not have believed in the religion he pro- fessed. "Should not religion enable a man to bear even ;so intense a trouble as this? What is the good of ; religious faith, if it break down here?" The answer to 1 that, at least, was this—that when reason was thrown from its seat, not even faith could prevail. "How could he hope," it was said, "for any mercy from God? Has Ihe not sealed his own eternal fate? Well there was 1 One that judgeth, and he had gone to that juster judge! It was no intention of his by making these references now to say a single word to lessen the natural power with which such a terrible tragedy should be regarded. If , the event should in any way contribute to the enhance- ment of life's sacredness in the mind of men, it might I serve even a valuable end. But he had felt it laid upon 1 him to give some expression to the deep pity that surely should be felt for a soul so crushed by uneadumble grief that it could do no other than force its way from , the earthly life. If the deed was the result of a deranged reason could either man or God hold the wretohed 1 doer responsible? Surelythat would be inconceivable. For himself (the preacher) he had hardly ever found it possible to think that suicide was compatible with ; absolute sanity. Especially such a case as this—inwhich ; grief was the unquestionable procuring cause. He I could, in some degrees,understand a man suddenly faced I with some calamitous exposure, which would entail upon him the merciless and expiable censure of the world, 1 rushing from the human tribunal and risking the Divine. 1 Such cases were on record, and he was not sure that there ' was not a very conspicuous one in the case of a military officer of renown, not many months ago. But this man I was overwhelmed, crushed, maddened by a bereavement ' and a grief that he could not bear. It was the language ,in the Book of Proverbs—" the spirit of a man will sustain his infirmity, but a woeided spirit who can bear ?" Surely some of them might have known or heard, of even devout Christian believers who bad found ; their reason tottering to its fall under the insupport- able weight of a great sorrow—under some devastatieg, desolating blow? Theyha,d been saved from it bysome • wise and timely interposition of watchfulness or restraint and gradually reconciled to their darkened , home and solitary life. He had himself been privy to a I sorrow of that kind in years gone by. and therefore he 1 could not bear to hear it said that this sad sufferer was undeserving of pity, and deserving only of reprobation 1 for his awful deed. The suggestion he felt called upon I to make was that Ms judgment was not left with us. Their deepest feeling concerning him should be that of Christien commiseratioß and compassion. We were not , all constituted alike. To every true and tender heart a I great bereavement was intensely hard to sustain. Some natures were more emotional than others. Love—which I bound two hearts together in the closest bond of life— could not endure the breaking of the bond without a , suffering that words could never tell. And if the pass- ing years had strengthened and intensified that marriage , bond by many a tie of gratitude, indebtedness, and devotion, its severance would, in some natures, prove a ; greater Anguish than could be borne. In this sad case there, were circumstances which had created very , peculiar and tender ties between those two souls. ' God only knows! And He alone is Judge. Surely our province was to be silent, if we could not say a pitying word. But if we felt we could pity a soul so demented by grief, let us give expression to it and help to put an end to the sharp censures and cold condenination6.which I others expressed.

===== 0002601_19030801_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
The Rev. F. E. Powell and the Tragedy.

Preaching on Sunday morning, at Sewerby, from the text "The Lord grant unto him that he may find mercy of the Lord in that Day; and in how many times he ministered unto, me at Ephesus thou knowest very well." —2 Tim. i. 18, the Vicar, the Rev. F. E. Powell, said : The fewest words are perhaps the best concerning the awful tragedy which has plunged two congregations--at Warrington and Sewerby—with many other personal friends into the most poignant grief. In the presence of an event so terrible,the heart seems instinctively to seek to express itself in the words of the Psalmist, who in the agony of his own sorrow, avers, "I held my tongue, and spake nothing; 'I kept silence even from good words, but it was pain 1 and grief to me." Those of us who knew the warmly affectionate, emotionally impulsive nature of Frederick Pearson need no assurance that only in a fit ' of the direst frenzy (in which we believe he was temporarily but absolutely irresponsible) could so rash an act, which his own normal conscience would not hesitate to denounce as shocking and wanton, be per- petrated. It is unspeakably sad to think that he who bore his own physical sufferings so cheerfully, should by his deeply acute grief in the loss of onehe so devotedly loved have become so completely unhinged in mind, ; that the restraints of reason conscience and religion I were disregarded. In the happening, therefore, of a tragedy so dark and perplexing,it is probably most • fittin,, for the deep and troubled thoughts of our hearts to find relief in the words of another Psalm: "I became even as a man that heareth not; and in whose mouth are no reproofs." "The Lord grant unto him that he may find mercy of the Lord in that Day !" But what for ourselves who have witnessed this spectacle of human infirmity which has so loudly called for our pity? Let us, one and all, "forbear to judge, for we are sinners all." But at the , same titne let us, one and all, take solemn heed and warning. No attempt, however sincere and well-inten- tioned, must be made, or even appear to be made, to disguise from us the awfulness of the sin of self-destruc- tion. The sacredness of human life, the wonderful dignity of human nature, the infinite mystery which hovers over a man's personality—and since Christ has exalted humanity on high —the magnificent potentiality of a human soul, makes the enforcement of the sixth commandment , so necessary that no thought of endearment which binds ; us to anyone whatever,must be allowed in any way or ; degree to lessen its cogency or power for us. Before God I dare not say yes ; before my fellow-men I will( not say more. Questions concerningany particular cage i which are bound to agitate,to perplex, almost to madden I us we will make no attempt to answer; we leave them to Him Who only can resolve them. Sincere regard and deep compassion for one whom we love must not blind us to the solemn duty we owe to the Church, and to the community at. large in which these terrible episodes are becoming alarmingly prevalent.

Other References.

The services at St. James' Church on Sunday morn- ing and evening were conducted by the former curate, the Rev. A. N. Taylor, and were well attended. No ser- mon was preached but Mr. H. N. Houghton, one of the wardens, had received a very sympathetic letter from the Rev. R. Greenall, rector of Grappenhall and patron of the living, with reference to the death of the Vicar and Mrs. Pearson. This was read. The itector, who is on a visit to Windermere, wrote: " I hAve heard of the awful calamity which has be- fallen the parish. Poor Mr. Pearson appears to have broken down mentally through anxiety on Mrs. Pearson's account. The sad misfortune has affected me most deeply, and I feel sure that you and all the congregation are in a like position with myself. In such cases we feel so helpless, so utterly incapable of giving any aid, or suggesting any alleviation. Kind regards to yourself and any of the congregation who may be pleased to know that I am in sympathy with them in-their trouble, and pray for them that Gainey mercifully allow good to come from the eadand moving calamity which has come upon St. James's -Parish."
