MRS. GROSFIELD'S GIFT TO WARRINGTON.

Opening of the "Crosfield Conservatory." Mrs. John Crosfield,formerly of Walton Lea,butnovv re- siding at Colwyn Bay,paid a visit to Warrington on Tues- day, and very gracefully performed the opening ceremony in connection with the conservatory she has presentedto the town,and which in future will be known as the "Crosfield Conservatory." The conservatory is the outcome of a gift of camellias which Mrs. Crosfield desired to make to the town. The Corporation found themselves unable to house the plants in the gardens, and Mrs. Crosfield then kindly offered to build a con- servatory at her own expense. The work was commenced without delay,and Messrs. Halliday & Sons, of Middleton near Manchester,are to be congratulated on the beautiful structurethey,have erected. It occupies the site of the old vinery house,on the Kendrick-street side of the gardens, and forms a pleasing addition to the attractions of the park. Thecamellia plants, about 30 in number,have been carefully arranged and planted by Mr. Parker, the head gardener to the Corporation. All thoplants,with the excep- tion of twohavestood the change,and therelis every/pros- , pect of the plants thriving. In addition to the camellias in the new conservatory, there are also to be seen a lapageria, presented by the Mayor and another by Alder- man Bolton, as well as a number of flowering plants which have been placed in the semi-circular part of the conservatory near to the principal entrance. A large gathering assembled to witness the opening ceremony. As a souvenir of the occasion, the Corpora- tion presented Mrs. Crosfield with a solid silver rose bowl, of early Chippendale pattern, and bearing the arms of the borough beautifully enamelled thereon. The inscription surrounding thearms read as follows "To Mrs. John Crosfield, from the Corporation of War- rington, on the occasion of the opening by her of the Crosfield Conservatory, presented to her by the town 28th July, 1903. J. CHARLTON PARR, Mayor." Messrs. Eustance and Company, of Sankey-street, supplied the bowl.

Mrs. Crosfield (who was accompanied by Mr. Arthur H. Crosfield, Miss Crosfield, and Mrs. Hales) were received on Tuesday afternoon in the vestibule at the Town Hall at three o'clock by the Mayor and Mayoress. Amongst others_ present at the time were Mr. Roger Parr, Alderman Bolton (chairman of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee, Alderman Smethurst (deputy chairman), Aldermen Fairclough, Hutchinson, Bennett, and Heaton; Councillors Brophy, Ellison, James Evans, and John Roberts; the Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle), Mr. Woodcock (assistant treasurer and borough collector), Mr. Charles Madeley (curator and librarian), Major Roome, Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, Mr. Willis, Mr. John Harrison, Mr. Kipps (Mrs. Crosfield's head gardener at Colwyn Bay), and Mr, Parker,the Warrington Corporation head gardener,and a number of well-known local ladies. Letters of apology for non-attendance had been received from Aldermen Francomb and Monks, Councillor Dodds, Mr. George F. F. White, and the Borough Treasurer. Alderman Francomb wrote :—"I very much regret that a previous engagement in Manchester will prevent me attending the opening of the'Crosfield Conservatory' by Mrs.' Crosfield on Tuesday. I feel convinced that such spontaneous generosity on the part of Mrs. Crosfield will ever be thoroughly appreciated by the people of Warrington."

PRESENTATION OF THE CONSERVATORY. The party assembled in the Council Chamber where the presentation was made.

The proceedings were presided over by the Mayor who asked Mrs. Crosfield to make the presentation. ' Mrs. CROSFIRLD, who had a cordial reception, spoke as follows :—I was very pleased when the Town: Hall, Parks and Baths Committee accepted the gift of the camellias. I wish to thank them, especially Mr. Lyon Whittle, Mr. Longdin, and Alderman Bolton for the kind and ready help they have given me in the matter. This park, as most of you know, was the gift of the late Mr. George Crosfield, and when he lived at Walton Lea. he took a great interest in these same camellia trees It was he who first started growing them there,nearly 40 years ago. It, therefore, seems ame that it is most fitting they should find a home here. lam very glad to hear that with the exception of two, all the trees have stood the change, and are doing well, and I have no doubt that' under Mr. Parker's good care they will blossom quite as well in the town as they used to do in the country. I have been very pleased to provide a house for them, and think you will agree with me that Mr. Halliday is to be congratulated on the suitable and well-built conservatory he has erected. (Hear,hear, and applause.) lam in hopes that this house mayprove the beginning of something larger and better, and that in time to came Warrington may have winter gardens such as so many of us have seen and enjoyed in foreign 'towns, and which are now becoming general at many of our seaside resorts. It is a project which Mr. Lyon Whittle, Mr. Longdin and others, who wish for the wel- fare of Warrington, and the beautifying of our smoky town (forgive the expression) have long had in their minds, and would like to see realized. I have very I greet pleasure in opening this conservatory, and I hope I it may give enjoyment to many of our townspeople, especially to our poorer townspeople, whose lives are passed in surroundings not so bright as some of us have the good fortune and happiness to enjoy. The MA.YOR, in accepting the gift, said he had the honour and the pleasure, of a.coepting, as Mayor, on behalf of the town, that magnificent colaservatory ; they might call it a miniature Crystal Palace. (Hear, hear.) Ile did so with a great and a deep sense of grati- tude, first as a most handsome and welcome addition to the park and gardens, and next as evidence of the kindly interest Mrs. Crosfield still took in Warrington awl its surroundings. She and her family had removed to a fresher and a more congenial atmosphere, where flowers grew and flourished in the open without the assistance of a glasshouse. He ventured to say thatat Colwyn Bay even camellias might grow in the open air, as he knew they did in many parts of Herefordshire, though in their industrial centre, and in that somewhat murky atmos- phere, they required to be protected by glass. It was I not only for the house theyhad to thank Mrs. Crosfield but for the camellia plants, which had such a suitable, excellent, and appropriate home. it would be in the recollection of many present, and of many who were not present, but for whom he felt he might speak very con- fidently,with what pleasure and enjoyment they welcomed the days when the late Mr. John Crosfield opened his gardens to the public to give them an opportunity of enjoying the sight of his beautiful flowers, and notably Qf the camellias, now the property of the town and the inhabitants. (Ap,nlense.) They all recognized the noble and refitting eifects of the study of nattzreand hor- ti.talture, and whereas through the oaprioious and uncer- tain character of their climate and the lack of sunshine especially in winter, plants were impossible of culture in the open, they had in the beautiful conservatory in the gardens a means of producing and growing plants, which he was sure, would be keenly appeciated by the population of the town, especially by the poor, who had no other opportunity of seeing and admiring them. (Hear,hear,and applause.) In accepting the gift on behalf of the town he did so with a deep sense of the kindness and generosity which bad prompted Mrs. Crosfield to make it, and to assure her that it would rank among the many acts of kindness for which the town was so much indebted to the Crosfield family, past and present. (Ap- plause.) Might he also express the hope, which he knew would be shared by every man, woman, and child in Warrington, that Mrs. Crosfield might often favour them with her presence, and so be able to recognize by personal experience the pleasure and enjoyment her gift would afford to the appreciative public of the Borough withip the gardens ? (Applause.)_

===== 0002601_19030801_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
A SOUVENIR: FOR MR:S.atRaVIELD.

The TOWN CLERK then placed the rose bowl on the table in front of the Mayor, Mayoress, and Mrs. Crosfleld.

The MAYOR hoped he would be pardoned for rising again, but he had another pleasing duty to perform. He had been requested by the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee to ask Mrs. Crosfield's acceptance of a com- memorative token of that memorable day. It was felt she would appreciate a little souvenir from the town to mark the day, and that it could not take a more appropriate and acceptable form than a flower bowl with a suitable inscription. They hoped it might often have the chonour of receiving from her hands its com- plement of roses On other flowers, and that when she arranged them she might remember Warrington, and think of the pleasure and enjoyment her kind munific- ence had provided not only forthe people of the present, but for the generatioas yet to come. (Applause). Mrs. CROSFIELD, in reply, thanked the Mayor extremely for the beautiful gift which had been given to her to remind her of that interesting occasion. She did not require anything to remind her of the town in which she bad spent so much of her life, and which would always hold so high a place in her affections. She would always treasure that charming bowl as a souvenir of her connection with Warrington as well as for its own sake, and for the kind thought which prompted them tq give it her. (Applause.) VOTE OF THANKS TO THE MAYOR.

Alderman BOLTON, in proposing a vote of thanks te the Mayor for presiding, and for making the presenta- tion, said he did not think they could have had a better I chairman. The committee put the right num in the , right place when they asked him to attend on that occa- sion. (Hear, hear.) The committee heartily thanked Mrs. Crosfield for having given them the camellias, and he could assure her that they would do ' their best for them. He believed camellias would grow in a smoky town' and he hoped that they would be as successful with them in Warrington as Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield were successful at Walton Lea. The house was a credit to the gardens, and to the town,and the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee would do their best to make it a credit to all concerned. (Applause.) Alderman SMETHURST seconded the vote of thanks and said they all felt highly indebted to Mrs. Crosfield for her handsome gift. It was appreciated by all in the town, high and low, rich and poor. (Applause.) The motion MIS heartily agreed to. The MAYort, in acknowledging the compliment. said he had seldom been in a more enviable or delightful position, and that was to be the mouthpiece of a grate Jul town in accepting a most magnificent present, which- would not only be a charm and pleasure to the present generation but many generations to come. (Applause.) The party then proceeded to the conservatory, Mr. Crostleld being accompanied by the Mayoress. Mrs. Crosfield expressed herself delighted with the conserva- tory and with the general arrangements.. A tour was 1 mile round the park, and on teterning to the Town Hall,tea was served in one of the committee rooms.

THE EXAMINER.

AUGUST Ist 1903.

THERE is one thing THE GOVERNMENT that cannot be

AND DEAR SUGAR. denied to Mr. BALFOUR'S adminis- tration. It has deserved and will obtain a unique place in history as the administration which systematically aimed at the subordination of the wide interests of the community to those groups of individuals who alleged that they were being subjected to some sort of "intolerable strain.' There is the case of the West Indian sugar planters, whom the Sugar Convention Bill is to benefit to the extent of some quarter of a million a year. It is contended by the Government that the West Indian planters are being destroyed by the bounties on sugar. If that were the case,it would pay us much better to pension them eft on a scale of extreme liberality than to legislate as we are doing on the lines of the Sugar Convention Bill. For what is it that will take place under that measure ? The distressed sugar planten in the West Indies may profit by our rejection of bounty- fed sugar to the extent of a quarter of a million annually,but in order to bring about this result we shall have to penalise ourselves to the extent of a sum variously estimated at six, seven, or eight millions annually. We are to pay seven or eight million pounds a year, that is to say, in order to give the West Indies a mere fraction of that amount. The Government affirms that this is the only manner in which the West Indies can be helped. But the best available testimony points to [a different conclusion. The West Indian planters can be best helped by a determination to help themselves. They had the example of Cuba before them. Cuba has gone through a long period of storm and stress, of blood and fire, but since the war was concluded it has plunged eagerly into industrial pursuits, and especially into the production of sugar. It is vastly increasing its;exports,whilst those of the West Indies are at a standstill, the increase in Cuba being, of course, a conclusive enough proof that the industry is being carried on there at a profit. The West Indian sugar planters- lag behind their Cuban competitors because they have less energy, less enterprise, and an ignoble sense of dependence on the Imperial Government, produced for the most part, it must be said, by Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S extravagant style of treating the extremities of the Empire,as if they were of infinitely more importance than the major part. The West Indies, said Mr. HUDSON KEA.RLEY on Wednesday—and the member for Devonport is peculiarly well qualified to speak with authority on a subject of this kind—have all the material advantages for growing sugar profitably; but, he added, nothing will save the sugar' planters unless they improve their methods. There is the whole matter in a nut- shell. The planters are enervated by the climate. They will not put forth the extra exertion needed to make their industry profitable, and they frighten Mr. CHAMBERLAIN by talking of making overtures to the United States. Hence the disastrous bargain that an incompetent and unforeseeing Government is determined to force upon the country ; against all manner of warning and good advice. That it may help the West Indies to compete under obsolete conditions and by the old. and unprofitable methods, it is taking steps to destcoy some of our own recent and most profit- able industries, to increase the price of one of the most beneficial and most necessary articles of food, and to compel us to tax sugar imports at the dictation of an international commission.

An attempt is made to secure the support of Protectionist opinion by representing that the free importation of bounty-fed sugars, has des- troyed, or is destroying, our sugar refining indus- try. This is an old contention. It led Lord SALISBURY to write his famous letter to "My dear Peter," many years ago. The experience of every year that passes tends further towards its refuta- tion. Mr. KEARLEY was able to show in his masterly and very informing speech on Wednesday that we are refining more sugar than formerly. There are fewer refineries; that is true, but those which exist are carrying on their business on the gigantic scale. The firms of Tate and Lyell turn out as much refined sugar as thirty refineries did in previous years. But to show the unsoundness of the views on which the legislation of the present Government is based avails nothing. To prove that the general body of people at home will Stan' from this legislation avails nothing. The Govern- ment has its majority behind it, and is absolutely without scruple as to the purpose for which it may be employed.

Sir WILLIAM A REBUFF FOR THE HARCOURT was back RAND MAGNATES. in his place in the

House of Commons on Alonday, and his reappearance after two months' absence was hailed with satisfaction on both sides. Mr. CII/MBICRLAIN took occasion to congratulate the veteran statesman on behalf of the supporters of the Government, and the hearty cheers of the Liberal members when he seated him- self on the Front Bench, repeated when ho rose to speak on the South African War Contribution Bill, testified to the undiminished regard in which Sir WILLIAM is held by the party which he formerly led. It soon became evident that during the period of his absence he has kept his mind closely fixed on the doings of Parliament, and on the proceedings of the Government especially in respect to South Africa. His remarks on the Rand labour questions drew from Mr. CHAMBERLAIN two very important declara- tions. Lord MILNER, as is well-known, has

===== 0002601_19030801_art0050.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
I;ffi

===== 0002601_19030801_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
_ - -

requested the Colonial secretary to use his influ-

ence with Lord GEORGE HAMILTON in order to induce the Secretary for India to permit of the importation of 10,000 coolies for work on the new railways in South Africa. It is a matter of still greater notoriety that the mine-owners are hankering after Chinese cheap labour for the mines, and are pressing the Government to con- sent to the importation of Chinamen. The general impression has been that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN would yield to both demands; but we are glad tobe able to congratulate the Colonial Secretar on the fact that he has not given way to influences which, as Sir WILL/AM HARcouRT plainly hinted, have Stock Exchange con- siderations behind them. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN announced that before he consents to the employ- ment of Asiatic labour in the Transvaal,he must have some reasonable proof that the policy is one of which the people of the new colony approve. The proof happens at present to be wholly on the other side. The people of the Transvaal heartily disapprove of the importation either of coolies or of Chinese; and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN says he will respect the opinion of the Colony, though it is quite evident that he does so with reluctance. Why, asks the Colonial Secretary, should coolie labour, the labour of our own fellow subjecte, be refused in a British Colony ? The question can be answered in many ways and from several points of view, but it should be sufficient to say that thet proposed importation of coolies is a device for depressing the wages of labour and for confining the Kaffir labourers to the mines. If the policy is to be resisted by the Government, as Mr. CHAMBERLAIN promises, until the Colony has changed its opinion on the subject, the question will settle itself in the natural way,by wages adapt— ing themselves to such labour as is at present obtainable in South Africa.

The question about Chinamen, says the Colonial Secretary, is premature, and he adds that at pre- sent no suggestion has bean made to him as to the employment of Chinese by the Transvaal Govern- ment. This is rather evading the real. issue. The proposals for importing Chinamen do not come from Lord MILNER,but from the capitalists of the Rand. However, as the promise given by Mr. CHAMBERLAIN applies no less to Chinamen than to coolies,lthe mine owners may now perhaps take the hint that they must conduct their business in a business-like way. Their troubles in regard to labour in the mines are to a very large extent of their own making. Before the war they were telling their share- holders what large sums, amounting to millions of pounds, they would be able to save on the wages bill,if the Boer Government ,were out of the way- When the war came to an end they were guilty of the almost incredible fatuity of reducing wages by' one-half. They found, as they might have 'antici- pated, that the natives would not work on such terms. Even if there had been no war it is doubt- ful whether,the KAFFIRS who had been working in the mines would not have gone back to their kraals When wages were :so largely reduced;. but through the war they had learned what 10 the :English idea of the equivalent of a day's labour. They had been well-paid for working above ground, and they declined to go into the mines for less than one-fourth of what they had been paid by the Government during the continuance of hostilities. The sensible course for the mine owners to pursue would have been to slightly raise the wages of Kaffir labour when 00 mines were reopened. The dividends could very well afford it. They chose to take the opposite course, believing no doubt that the Government would countenance any measures which seemed necessary for the provision of cheap labour. There is reason to suspect that they have represented the labour diffic•ilty as being much greater than lt really is,in order to get the Government behind. them. But so far they have only been able to bring round Lord MILNER on their views. gr. CHAMBERLAIN BtalldS by the opinion of the people of the Colony, and the Rand mine owners will therefore be compelled to make the best term' they can with the K*ffirs.

Mr. Henry Thornton has at last succeeded ie entering the Town Council and he is to be con- gratulated on the achievernewt, especially as he has triumphed by so large a majority as 31:1 The bulk of the congratulation, however, appears to us to be due to the Non-Trading Association, which has added one more proof that it has the majority' of the people at its back. The Non-Traders have apparently, only to give a candidate their hall- mark of genuineness as an apostle of the Creed, and hey presto !he is elected. Why it is almost as easy for men to enter the Council now—if the) are bona-fide Non-Traders—as it was.in the days of the notorious compact. So far Mr. Dodds and his friends have not lost a seat they have set out to capture, and judgir4; by appearances they have. the fate of the November elections in the hollow of their hand. Whether the inquiry is under way or not before then, there is little likelihood of it being delayed long after the elections. The- Non-Traders look like being in a position to dictate before the end of the year.

The "Crosfield Conservatory" which was formally opened to the public of Warrington on Tuesday is another rernieder of the many beneficent acts which have endeared the Crosfield family to the town. We are indebted to the late Mr. George Crosfield for the beautiful park in which the conservatory stands, and there are several other local institutions which bear witness to the generosity of the late Mr. John Crosfield- The public benefactors of Warrington have never at any period in its history been ing.ny in number but the townspeople do not readily torget the honoured names of the generous few. Tnere was peculiar fitness in a Mayor bearing the name of Parr, being the unouth-Oece of a grateful toiv.ri in accepting Mrs. Crosfield s generous gift. It 16 some consolation to the public who have been de' prived of the pleasure of visiting Walton Lea to see the choice collection of plants and flowers which were so often on view there, to know that the camellia plants are now the property of the Corporation and can be seen in the Park. If anything could add to the pleasure of receiving Mrs. Crosfield's gift it viiis the graceful way in which she made it. "). am in hope," she said,"that this conservatory EnaY prove the beginning of something larger and better, and that in time to come Warrington may havo winter gardens." Mrs. Crosfield has given expres" sion to a project which has long been in the min=lo of those who are desirous of brightening the sar.- roundings of the people who have to spend their lives in this busy industrial centre. The realiss- of it must be left to private benevolence, which we trust will not be wanting now that Mrs. Cros- field has so admirably shown the way to the charitably—inclined.

He has apparently found a man a good -deal after hiE own heart in Mr. Slots, Secretary of the Trades Council, and with him agrees that 'Protection will put oft the day when labour the world over will recognize the fact that it must be united against the common enemy, the capitalist." This is probably true enough, but we attach even "more importance to Mr. Whitham's words when he gives the following advice to electors: "You must send men to Parliament who will not turn back th 3 fingers of time on an outrageous Education Bill. He is not a militant member at :any time, and the most enlightened and energetic of Tory M.P.'s usually fight shy of these ,two subjects. They are much more conveniently kept in the background. The mono-rail has evidently nit commended "itself to the investing public. A circu'ar has been issued by the directors announcing "that the applications for shares do not, in their judgment offer capital of a sufficient amount to guarantee the successful oompleuion of so important an 'undertaking. Arrangements for the piovision of its full resources required are under considera- tion." . This means a serious postpone- ment of the work,and the mono-rail is hardly likely to be seen in our midst yet awhile. It is unfortunate that the appal to the public has met With so poor a response, for the scheme was one calculated to prove, we believe, not only an immense convenience between two of the first cities in the country, but a commercial success also. The mono-rail may yet mark the dawn of a new era in railway travel and we much regret that the day has been indefinitely postponed. It seems fairly clear that there has been no lack of prospec- tive small investors, but that the men with plenty of spare capital are indisposed to put their money in the new scheme. Probably many of them have capital invested in existing railways, and they may have thought that to support the mono-rail was to 'depreciate shares which they already held else- where. But whatever the cause,the capital' has not been forthcoming and we shall have to wait until the promoters can devise some other means of securing the financial aid necessary to make a atm

• Mr. T. J. DJWII in his speech on the compensation question at the meeting of the district representa- tives of the National Trade _Defence Association Was unusually frank. In pleading the cause of Compensation for the withdrawal of licences it is Usual to point to the injustice and hardship Which such action brings upon the "poor publi- can." The Government has already turned a eYmpathetic ear to this plaintive note, and we are sesured that the Government will introduce and Pass a measure in the next session of Parliament Providing for compensation to licence-holders Whose licences have been withdrawn through 120 fault of their own. Mr. Down now comes forward with the suggestion that "the trade" must see that the compensation tees further than that. It is not only :se "poor publican" who must receive compensa- 00a but els) the "rich brewer." This is what we anticipated, and it will be found that the strongest eupporters of compensation are persons connected With wealthy brewing companies. In most cases , the publican is a mere servant of the -brewing company in a tied house and he cannot hope for any large amount of compensation. The plum will be for the owners, who are invariably the rich brewing companies. This is what has been very inaptly called "equitable compensation." Fancy prices are still being given by brewers for licensed houses, despite "the confiscation craze," in the hope of compensation. There is a chance of them being disappointed, and in the interests of the uation it is to be hoped they will be. Before the Government begins to make grants to the brewer and the publican it had better put the House in something like order for the incoming tenants. I-- One never realizes more fully what aa excellent 'work the N.S.P.C.A. is doing than when such cases .of cruelty to animals are brought to light as were heard at Frodsham Petty Sessions on Wednesday. Two Frodsham farmers were charged with ill- treating horses in their possession, and the 'evidence in each ease was such as to fill one with Ti unholy desire to have five minutes with the 'culprits themselves. Henry Savage—appropriately lamed—bad thrashed his horse with a whip until broke,and then with a buckle. The poor brute suffered so much in consequence of its illtreatment sod a dislocated leg that it had to be shot. George :Okell, the second defendant, had a horse that Jibbed. His cure was to light a batten of straw beneath she. horse's stomach and raise a blister eighteen inches long by a foot wide. This is corporal punishment with a vengeance. We only 'regret that the magistrates were so lenient as to :let the defendants off with a fine. A month's bard labour would have been none too severe a 'judgment upon two such brutal individuals.

No one can wonder at the disaffected condition *Of Russia who reads about the police supervision -Of the schoolboys. The theory that Socialism lakes its root in the childish mind while in the early teens, is an obsession of the officials, and youths are held up for examination as to their political Opinions before they are allowed to enter a ITniver- Any. Their private 'parties and gatherings are Watched, on the assumption that they are 411 Anarchists; and concealment and secrecy and all the other dark arts are thus taught them at the *beginning. There can be hut one result from this effort to crush the spirit. Youth will be 'Served, and if it is taught to re.zard political 'discussions as a danger, a sheer love of *adventure will spur lads on to community of f action. These schoolboy conclaves will nave nothing in them of the nature of a conspir- 'acy ; but the lads will be trained to love darkness :rather than light, and plot and cabal mysteriously. They mty also be tempted to ask themselves Whether there is not something very rotten in a government which is afraid of the united judg- ,lnent of a pack of schoolboys. This sort of thing does not' lead up to respect or reverence for age or %uthority. On the co'ntrary it excites a belief that t!?e political system will not stand investigation. Fhe boys become moody, sulky, rebellious. This la exactly the temper that wise statesmanship ohould endeavour to avoid, if statesmen will inter- 'fere with the duties of parents.

At Lancaster, on Thursday morning, Frank Linton, newsboy, was sent to a reformatory for four years for stealing a tin trunk and wearing apparel belonging to May Kendall, of Barrow, who had come to Lancaster on a holiday. Sudden Death of a Retired Farmer at Lymm. An inquest wasiheld at the Plough Hotel, Lymm, on Wednesday evening, by Mr. Fisher, bit° the circum- stances attending the death of William Moreton, a retired Farmer of Whitegate, Statham Lymm. Nellie Moreton, daughter of the deceased, gave evidence of identification. Her father was 68 years of age.—Kate Knowles, a domestic servant in the employ of Mr. Moreton stated that .the deceased was working in the garden as usual on Tuesday. About noon she heard him fall in his bedroom and on going into the room saw him lying face downwards. She went to a neighbour's house, and Mr. Astbary rendered assist- ance. A. doctor was sent hr but the deceased was found to be dead.—Dr. E. M. de Jong, practising at Lymm, deposed that the deceased died of heart trouble.—A verdict that the deceased died of sudden heart failure was returned.

Gallant Rescue from Drownini. The canal bridge at Bewsey was the scene,on Mon- day evening of a gallant rescue from drowning. A girl named Kennedy, nine years of age, who resides with her parents at 12, Pierpoint-street, was standing on the bridge nursing a two-year-old baby, when she accidentally lost her hold of the child, and it slipped into the water. In trying to reach it she oveclrdanced, and a moment later she also was struggling in the water. Some children playing near gave an alarm and a man named Gibson, who is in the service of the London and North-Western Company, at once jumped into thin canal and succeeded in rescuing both children, who were thoroughly exhausted. We learn that this is the second time during the past week that Gibson has rescued persons from drowning at Bewsey, and it is to be hoped that the Royal Humane Society will not fail to hear about it.

The Warrington Poor Boys' Camp.

The second- detachment Of boys, 81 in number, returned home yesterday after a week's experience of camp life at Leasowe Common, Wallasey. The weather has been rather of an unsettled character,but nevertheless the boys enjoyed the change very much. The detachment has been in charge of Messrs. W. H. J. Jenkins (secretary), Fred J. Knutsford Liberal Association Picnic. A picnic of the officers and friends of the above association was held a fortnight ago, the destination b3ing Styal. About 150 attended and the walk through Oversley Woods was much enjoyed. Tea was partaken of at ths Ship Inn, followed by a brief but enthusiastic meeting. Mr. Henry Phillips Grey presided and spoke encouragingly of the prospects of the party in the Knutsford. Division. Mr. Alfred J King, the Liberal candidate for Knutsford Division,. delivered a thoughtful speech on the Tariff Question which proved the soundness of the principles •of Free Trade, especially so seeing that Mr. King is a leading cotton manufacturer at Bellington, employing hundreds of workmen.---Mr. J.A. Thompson proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. King for his address and pledging those present to use legitimite means to secure Mr. King's return as Liberal member for Knutsford Division. This was seconded by Mr. E. Lloyd-Jones, Stockton Heath, and carried unani- mously.

PUBLIC BFINEFIT. UNDER the CLOCK BEST for BOOTS in LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Mishap to a Warrington Train. What might have proved a very serious accident, happened at Chester Station on Wednesday night between. eight and nine o'clock. . After the Lime-stree (Liverpool) train, due at 8.15, had entered the short bay on No. 3 platform, and all the passengers had de- trained, the Warringtou train, which arrived shortly after 8.32, entered the bay and collided with the rear of the Liverpool train, badly damaging a van and composite compartment of the latter train. Fortun- ately, the incoming passenger train approached the bay at slow speed, and beyond one or two of the passengers in the front carriages of the Manchester train complaining of shock, and the damage to thsl Liverpool train, nothing more resulted. from the collision. The damaged carriages were shunted into a siding. The end of each coach was smashed to splinters, while the buffers were twisted almost off, and the axle boxes and springs damaged, and wood- work split. Three windows in a third-class compart- ment were smashed. The engine of the Warrington traiu. was considerably damaged, the front buffers be- ing twisted and sand boxes displaced. The Roman Remains at Warrington.

A large party of members of the—Lancashire and Cheshire Antiquarian Society made an excursion to Warrington on Saturday for the purpose of examining the foundations of a house recently uncovered on the, site of the Roman civitas at Stockton Heath, which is being explored at the expense of the Museum Com- mittee'. Mr. Thomas May acted leader and imparted a great deal of useful i nformation. Mr. Charles Madeley curator and. librarian also accom- panied the party, After carefully examining the foundations of the house at Stockton Heath the party returned to the Old Academy for teo, an inspection of this ancient building being source of pleasure. Taking the tram to Latchford a visit was paid to Grappenhall Church where Mr. Mac read to the mem- bers of the society some notes which had been pre- pared by the Rector. The Roman font and other objects of interest were inspected. A visit was also paid to the Warrington Pariah Church. The Rector was unable to be present, but, like the Rev. R. G-reenall, he had prepared some notes which Mr. May read, giving a very interesting description of.the old , church. Altogether the visit to the district proved to be most instructive and enjoyable.

How he got into the canal is steaming,someone made the pleasant discovery that there unknown. was not enough coal on board and a delay of nearly three hours ensued. The scene on board was an amusing one, weary warriors lying in every part of the boat— on kit-bags, under benches, behind funnels and anyother convenient or inconvenient place. There was a. good number of cases of mel-de-mer. The Isle of Man was sighted about 7.0 o'clock and presented a very pretty spectacle, the summit of Snaefelrbeing wreathed in white fleecy clouds. Disembarkation took place at 8.30 and at about 9.30 the battalion started off for their camping ground about two miles from Ramsey along very mauddy road. However, they got there safe arra had breakfast,after the usual worry of getting kit-bagsd &c. At 12.30 a short service was held, conducted by the Rev.. W. Braceearnp, th 3 newly-appointed chaplain to the battalion. He urged his hearers to "Quit ye like men" during camp and carry it on through life. The officers present in camp are Majors Edelsten (corninanding)and Appleton, Captains J. D. Fairclough, B. Fairclough, G. R. Crosfield, Haslam, Williamson, and Roberts; Lieutenants Ridgway and Avery; Second Lieutenants Connor, Leighton, Fulton Smith, Roberts and Monks; Surgeon-Lieutenant, Curtis; Captain Leader (adjutant), and Quartermaster A. F. G. Potter. The total strength of the battalion is about 830 Non- Commissioned Officers and men. Included in the brigade are the Ist V.B. Royal Welsh Fusiliers, 2nd V.B. Cheshire Regiment and Ist V.B. Herefordshire Rifle Corps. The comforts of the men are well looked after, there being a large marquee belonging to the Y.M.C.A. on the groundwhere concerts are held every evening and letters can be written and games indulged in. Monday opened fineauddryand the battalion paraded at 9 o'clock in drill order and filled water bottles. Marching out of camp they made for Snaefell and formed up in assembly formation. They were, then broken up into companies and practised skirmishing. About 10 o'clock, the weather took a turn for the wors3 and the rain came down in torrents. The men stuck to their work and some very interesting operationstook place, Camp was reached at 12.30, everyone being wet through to the skin. No more parades took place that day, the rain coming down incessantly until after eight o'clock. The signallers and ambulance section were on brigade duty and the Maxim gnu section were engaged cleaning their guns. On Tuesday morning about 9 o'clock four companies went on the hills to the west of the camp and practised outpost duty and skirmishing. Captain B. Fairclough took the scouts out and didsome interesting operations and the rest of the battalion were practising company firing and judging distances. The signalling section went on Lewaigue Hill and practised with the trans- mitting station. The battalion, in thc afternoon prac- tised various movements in companies. The Mounted Infantrywere hurdle jumping and some of the men were thrown, but no one hurt. The machine gun section were firing on the hills to the west of the camp in the after- noon. The weather during the days, was all thatcould be desired and numbers of men left camp in the even- ing for Ramsey and Douglas. The battalion were all served out this day with shirts, belts, boots and slouch hats which will add to the smart appearance of the IneU generally. , A stiff day's work was experienced on Wednesday. "Fall in" sounded at 8.30 and the battalion paraded with the bands and the maxim gun. Refreshments were issued to the men in haversacks and filled water bottles were taken. Ten rounds of ammunition per man were taken. The men went along the road to Snaefell and opposed the Royal Welsh Fusiliers in a skirmishing manceuvre. The chief feature of the operations was the splendid way in which both sides found cover and carried out the passing of defiles, returning to camp at two o'clock. The signalling section were on brigade duty and did some very useful work. The officers of the battalion played a cricket .match with the gentlemen of Ramsey, and the match resulted in a win for Ramsey by 6 wickets. The different.companies are engaged in the prelimin- ary rounds of the tug-of-war contest, the final of which is included in the battalion sports, which will take place on Friday. In the evening the signalling section paraded for lamp practice. Thursday was another field day, parade being ordered at 8 a.m. The wind was blowing half a gale and many of the men when up the mountains found it hard work to keep their feet. The battalion was skirmishing and the signallers were scattered about the hills near the camp on brigade duty under the brigade signalling officer. In the afternoon some of the companies turned out in full marching order for inspection by the com- pany officers. RACES AND SPORTS YESTERDAY. WARRINGTON CHAMBER OF COMMERCE.

A meeting of the Council, was held at Market Grate Chambers, on Monday, the President (his Worship the Mayor) in the chair.

REDUCED RAILWAY FARES.

The SECRETARY (Alderman Bennett) read a letter from the Railway Clearing House in reply to a letter which had been forwarded, at the request ,of the Chamber, to all the local Railway Companies suggest- ing the desirability of making similar concessions in England to those enjoyed in Scotland in reference to cheap passenger tickets. It was stated that the matter had been carefully considered by the Railway Superin- tendents in conference, and that they regretted that they were unable to accede to the Council's request. MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S TARIFF' PROPOSALS.

The SECRETARY expressed his regret that the gentle- men whom it had been hoped would represent the Chamber at the forthcoming Congress of Chambers of i Commerce of the Empire n Montreal found that it would not be possible for them to do so, as the Congress would be specially interesting in view of Mr. Chamber- lain's proposals as to preferential trade, and would afford a unique opportunity to any member who could spare the time of visiting all parts of Canada. He said that many of the Chambers of Commerce of the United Kingdom were discussing Mr. Chamberlain's proposals and passing resolutions thereon, and suggested that, as the matter was of vital importance to the trade of the town, itwould be well to call a general meeting of the Chamber to discuss the subject. • The CHAIRMAN thought it WM better to wait until some more definite scheme was before the country. Messrs. C. E. Parker, Frederick Monks, John Fair- dough, J. Fulton Smith, H. P. Densham, and J. H. Smethurst, also spoke upon the subject, and it was finally resolved, on the motion of Mr. Parker, seconded by Mr. Faircloug.h "That the Executive of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce considers it premature to express any opinion on the important changes which Mr Chamberlain suggests in the fiscal policy of this country, but would view with satisfaction any thoroughly national enquiry into - the conditions under which our important international and colonial trade is carried on, and is prepared to offer any local facilities or help within its power." CONCILIATION TREATY WITH FRANCE. A resolution was also passed approving of the steps which have been taken by Nr. Thomas Barclay (ex-president of the British Chamber of Commerce in Paris) with a view to promoting a Conciliation Treaty between Great Britain and France.

MEAN THEFT BY A ST. REL6IS LODGER.

James Broom, a young, colliery worker, of Herbert- street, was charged at bt. Helens on Wedesday with stealing a number of articles, value 12s. from the honk() of Abel Jones, of 12, Southport-street. Mr. Wood, Chief Constable, said Broom went to. lodge at Jones's, but at the end of a week got into debt, and when he owed Ll for board and lodgings he went to Collins Green Colliery and draw his wages,and did not go back. Polioe-constable Tunstall arrested Broom, and found the articles on him. Broom said he was born on Aspull Moor. Fined 21s. or three weeks' imprisonment. MARRIED MISERY AT ST. HELENS.

Thomas Harrison,hawker,of Patricroft,was summoned at St. Helens on Wednesday for maintenance arrears. Mrs. Harrison said her husband had been all over the world. Hanging was too good for him. Harrison said he had no means of paying. He was sent to goal for two months.—Henry Lyon, collier, of 38, Cairns-street, was charged on a similar warrant. He pleaded that he would work and be teetotal, and on the understanding that he would pay something down he was liberated for a week. MARRIAGE OF MR. R. H. PILICGTON.

An interesting society wedding took place on Tuesday, at the Kensington Congregation Chapel, London, W., the contracting parties being Mr. R. H. Pilkington, of St. Helens. and Miss Hope Cozens-Hardy, younger daughter of Lord Justice Cozens-Hardy. The bride, who was given away by her father, wore a raisonette silk dress trimmed with Irish lace, with tulle veil, and was attended to the altar by six bridesmaids, the mem- bers of her ownand the bridegroom's family. Mr. Sidney rjlkington acted as best man. The Rev. Silvester- Horne, brother-in-law of the bride, officiated. After the ceremony a largely-attended reception was held at Bailey's Hotel. The wedding presents were numerous and handsome. MOTHER-IN-LAW DT PERIL.

Mary Walsh, a young married woman, appeared in the dock at St. Helens on Tuesday with a baby in her arms, and was charged with a breach of the peace. Police- constable Bramwell said the woman was creating a riot and breaking the windows of the model lodging-house kept by her mother-in-law. Some timeago the prisoner was sent to gaol for neglect of children. Mrs. Walsh, sen., took charge of two of the children, and prisoner was tryingto get her children back.—Accused said that hor husband was ill-treating her, ancihe flung the brush at him, but missed him and struck tfie window.— Bramwell said the husband was perfectly sober, and in bed at the time.—The magistrates advised the w011),R11 tO go to the workhouse, and discharged her.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

SMALLPDX AT WIDNES.

We hear that Mr. Isaac Carr, gas and water engineer, is suffering athis residence from an attack of smallpox. Mr. Carr went out last Tuesday week with the members of the Council on their annual inspection,and was work- ing on Thursday at the gasworks. How he contracted the malady is not known. SALVAGrAWARD.

On Wednesday, in the Admiralty Court, Mr. Justice Walton and Trinity Masters awarded £1,600 salvage to the owners of the steam tug Blazer for the salvage of the steamship Duncrag off the coast of Anglesey in July last. The ship was bound with a cargo of flints from 106 camp to Weston Point, on the Mftnchester Ship Canal.

A VIOLENT NAVVY AT WIDgES.

'On Thursday morning, George Jout, a labourer work- ing on the Vyrnwy pipe track at Growood, was charged with assaulting George Whitfield and his wife on Satur- day last. Prisoner went into the Growood Tavern and asked for drink, but was refused owing to his condition and was asked to leave the house, whereupon he became violent and struggled, kicked the landlord in the face, and blackened his eye. Eventually he was got off the premises, but soolteturned, and assaulted the landlady also giving her allack eye. Prisoner, in his defence pleaded that he had been assaulted also. The bench sent him to gaol for one month with hard labour. Mr. Bacon, the chairman, said they were determined to pro- tect publicans who tried to conduct their businesses properly. ASS AU LT ON A RUNCORN PUBLICAN.

On Monday at the Runcorn Petty Sessions, James Robinson, Mill-street, was charged with having assaulted. Luke Holmes, licensee of the Anchor Inn, Brunswick- street, on July 24th. He pleaded guilty.—Complainant said the prisoner came into the house at half-past ten on the night of the 24th July. He was drunk, and asked to be served. He was told he had had too much drink and that he must go outside. He refused. Com- plainant requested him to go several times. He commenced to quarrel with a customer, and complainant then put hint outside. He came in by another door, and said what he would do for complainant if he got six mouths. Complainant was going to the vaults and prisoner struck him a violent blow in the mouth. Prisoner • said he wanted to sell a medal belonging to the King for a shilling, when prosecutor threw him outside on his face. He re-entered the house by the next door, and prosecutor struck him. He then struck prosecutor. The magistrates committed • prisoner to gaol for a month and bound him over in two sureties for twelve months. SESSION'S MISCELLANY. At Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday before Messrs. A. Thomas and James Pritchard, Priscilla Hughes, Regent-street, was fined ss. and costs on the evidence of P.C. Preston for using obscene language in a court off Regent-street on July • 16th.—James Robinson and Annie Collins Mill-street, were charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Millstone-yard on July 9th. P.C. Wilding said the prisoners were drunk and behaved most scandalously. The neighbours strongly complained of their conduct. There were 23 previous convictions against Robinson and 26 against Collins. Robinson had been committed to gaol for a month and bound over in a previous sase. A month was added to his sentence. Collins was sent to gaol fore month and bound over for twelve months. —Minnie Price was fined 10s. and cos e.§ for having been drunk at the same time and place. P.C. Wilding proved the case. Elizabeth Chadwick. a tramp, against whom there were several convictions in other towns, was sent to gaol for 21 days for having been drunk and disorderly in Halton-road on Saturday night. P.C.Brayford proved the case. He said the woman refused to give her name. Superintendent Ennion said he saw the woman at the Police Station and recognized her as one who had been on tramp for eight years. She had been convicted several times at Altrincham and Over. RUNCORN LIBRARY COMM ITTE E. _ _

The monthly meeting of this committee was held at the Town Hall, Runcorn, last Friday evening. There were present :—Messrs. S. Pope (who presided), D.Bis- brown, T. Riley. W. Hankinson, F. W. Hutton, J. F, 1 Moulton, W. J. Cunningham, and Mrs. Jones (librarian.) The Chairman said the librarian's report showed an , average daily issue of 18 more than in the corresponding ' month of last year. The library had been open two days less than inJime last yea.r,butso more volumes had been taken out. The number of books borrowed was steadily increasing. In the Reference Department, however, there had been a decrease. The Chairman said the Book Selection Committee had drawn up a reply and for- warded it to Mr. Carnegie in answer to his sletter. The reply was signed by the Chairman of the Library Com- mittee and the Chairman of the Council. The Chairman .of the Council had received a reply stating that there was an item of expenditure under the head of rate. Mr.Carnegiecould not understand why this was so, when the buildings belonged to the Urban Council. The committee, in answer, pointed out that rate was only used as a convenient term and included lighting, rent, cleaning, repairs, &c. Photographs of the library buildings were also forwarded. Mr. 'Moul- ton asked if it would not be desirable to have Mr Carnegie's letter read for ths information of the Com- mittee. The Chairman said the letter was in the possession of the Chairman of the Council. The only point, Mr. Carnegie could not understand, was why they should pay rent when the premises belonged to the Council. Mr. Ilisbrown thought the outlook was more hopefnl since Mr. Carnegie had shown his interest to the extent of a second inquiry. Mr. Wilding had marked and endorsed the photograph.s showing the character of the buildings. After some discussion it was decided to defer the question of sending a representative to the Libraries' Associationronference at Leeds to the next meeting.

WINDOW SMASHING.

At Widnes, on Thursday morning, Michael Cummings, labourer, Lee-street, a retired army man, was charged with doing damage to the windows of the Blackburn Arms amounting to £3 10s. Prisoner pleaded guilty, but said he had been drinking for some days and did not know what he was doing. A fine of 10s. 6d. costs, and a damage was imposed, or in default fourteen days.

SHOCKING CRUELTY TO HORSES AT FRODSHAM.

IHenry Savage, farmer, Frodsham, was charged at Frodsham Petty Sessions on Wednesday with cruelly illtreating a horse at Frodsham on July 10th. Mr. Blake Jones, of the R.S.P.C.A., prosecuted. Alfred Thornley (16) said he was working with Mr. Savage in a field on the Marsh,mowing when one of the horses commenced to kick and got its leg over theshaft. The horses were taken out and witness went home with one of them. When he got back he saw Mr. Savage running the other horse round and beating it with a whip. He broke thewhip and then struck it several times with a buckle. He beat the animal for ' more tl4an half an hour. Witness last saw the horse at half-past five in the loose box. It was lying down with its head on the, floor. Sergeant Brs,tt said he heard of what had taken place and saw the defendant on the following night. He admitted that he thrashed the horse and that themiLa,d to shoot it. A Chester horse- slaufthterer had taken it away. Witness telephoned to the Chester police and the horse was deftained at Sal tney. Inspector Jones saw the body. Under the hide it was a mass of corruption from having been thrashed with a buckle. The near hind leg was dislocated. Mr. Churton, who defended, said defendant was obliged to thrash the horse to subdue it. A flue of 40s. and costs was impospd.—George- Okell, Such Farm, Frodsham, was summoned for a similar offence on July 23rd. The evidence of William and Robert Sutton, Sergeant Bratt and P.C.Proctor was to the effect that the horse gibbed in the field. Defen- dant first thrashed it with a stick and then placing a I batten of straw under its stomach set fire to it. Thel horse was severely burned. There was a blister eighteen inches long by a foot wide. Defendant was fined 40s. and 16s. 6d. costs. FRODSHAM PUBLICA.N FINED.

At Frodsham Petty Sessions on Wednesday before Messrs. A. Thomas (in the chair), J. Murray, J. Ockles- ton,and S. Woodward, Thomas Dykes, licensee of the Bridge Inn, Frodsham, was summoned for permitting drunkenness on his premises on the 16th ult.—Mr.Giles, of Chester, prosecuted for the police, andthe defend- ant was represented by Mr. Smith, of Liverpool, in- structed by Mr. Linaker. The evidence for the prosecution was to the effect that at half-past eight on the morning of the date mentioned,P.C. Proctor visited the house to serve some pig notices and saw a man named Charles Mills in one of the rooms. He was standing near the house at half-past eleven, and saw Mills come out. He was beastly drunk. He staggered across the road, and when he got to the bridge he fell down blokwards. He turned over and tried several times to 'et up, but was unable, and Proctor assisted him up. He put him against the bridge wall and went into the inn and saw a woman named Mary Morrison in the bar. He asked if Mr. Dykes was in. She replied that Mr. and Mrs. Dykes had gone to Rhyl, and added that she was is charge of the house. He told her that the man who had just left the house was in a beastly state of drunkenness. She said she was very sorry; she did not think he was so bad. She had served him with only one pint of beer.—For the defence Mr. Smith contended that the man had only one pint of beer in the house, and left at 10.45. He had been on the bridge some time when seen by Proctor. He was sober when he left the house. Six witnesses bore out this statement in evidence.—Defendant was fined 20s. and 16s. 6d. costs, And was ordered to pay the advocate's lee, 42 21.

• CHESHIRE EDUCATION. Grants by the Committee for Higher

Instruction.

A meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee Wag held at Crewe on Monday, Mr. C. B. Thornycroft pre- siding. The Clerk read some correspondence that had passed between Mr. J. H. Howitt, one of the newly- appointed Inspectors of Schools for the County of Chester, and himself, with regard:to his (Mr. Howitt's) resignation. Mr. Howitt applied to be allowed to withdraw his resignation' and on the motion of Dr. Hodgson, seconded by Mr. J. M. Yates, this was agreed to. With regard to the salariei of the clerks to the various committees, Dr. Hodgson said that as far as practicable the clerks would be re-appointed ; but their salaries would be fixed when it was ascertained what additional duties, if any, they are required to dis- charge. A discussion took place as to the appointment of a law clerk to advise the sub-committee for higher education for the Wallasey Urban district. Eventually the following resolution was passed unani- mously: "That the Wallasey Urban District Council be informed that the Cheshire Education Committee regret that they cannot recognise Mr. Cook as the legal adviser of the sub-committee, as the delegation by the County Council to the committee provided that all legal business in connection with the administration of the Act is to be conducted by, or under the supervision, of the clerk of the County Council."

The Higher Education sub-committee was appointed with Dr. Hodgson as chairman, and the Rev. W. H. Binney as deputy chairman. The dutiesassigned to the sub-committee included the provision of special classes, the making of gmnts,questions affecting the training of teachers, and the provision of suitable colleges for this work.

The question of taking over the Wallasey Grammar School was deferred.. The Director of Education was instructed to meet the Governors of the Nantwich and Acton Grammar School and discuss with them the question of the school being handed over to the County Council.

Grants were made to the following Higher Education Sub-6ommittee,s :—The boroughs of Congleton, £222 ; Crewe, £912: Dukinfield, £365; Hyde, £732.; Maccles- field, £668; Stalybridge, £449; and the Urban District of Wallasey, £1,260. Grants amounting to £3,534 were made to certain of the Elementary Education Adminis- trative Sub-committees for purposes of higher educa- tion. . _ _

Tt was decided that where vacancies for pupil teachers existed, the appointments should be advertised, so as to give all an equal chance of applying. A letter was read from the Education Department calling attention to the serious overcrowding of the Church School at Northenden, and intimating that if the recognised accommodation is again exceeded no further annual grant will be paid. The Clerk read a letter from the Sa.ndbach and Dis- trict Free Church Council complaining that of the 23 persons selected by the Administrative Sub-Committee of the Cheshire county Council to be managers of public elementary schools in the Congleton Union Dis- trict, 21 were members of the Church of England. Canon Armitstead explained how the appointments were made,

It was decided to furnish a copy of the county archi- tect's report on the condition of the elementary schools in the county area to the various school managers.

GIFT OF A SECONDARY SCHOOL.

Sir John T. Brunner, M.P., chairman of Brunner, Mond, and Co., has officially intimated that Dr. Ludwig Mond and himself will present to Northwich and the county of Cheshire a secondary school equipped for the teaching of 200 scholars. The Cheshire Education Com- mittee have made Northwich a centre for higher educa- tion, and this joint gift will relieve the county of considerable expense. The school will be worked in conjunction with the Verdin Technical School, North- wich, where certain secondary classes can be held.

THE MONO-RAIL Failure to Raise the Capital. No Shares to be Issued.

The Manchester and Liverpool Electric Express Rail- way Company have issued the following circular, bearing the sigsature of Mr. J. It. Swan, secretary, to all appli- cants for shares in the company : "I am instructed by the Board of Directors to inform you that the applications received for shares in the above company do not, in their judgment, offer capital of a sufficient amount to guarantee the successful com- pletion of so important an undertaking. "Arrangements for the provision of the full resources required are under consideration, but meanwhile they think it advisable to allot no shares at the present stage. The directors have arrived at this conclusion with regret. " The 'numerous applications received from small local investors make them desirous to further, if possible, the commencement of a public enterprise which has been approved by the joint committee of representatives of 3faechester and Liverpool, by the Committee of the House of Commons, by the Board of Trade, and by the most eminent electrical engineers. The Act incorporating the Company has left to the discretion of the directors the minimum amount upon which shares may be allotted, and they have concluded that any deficiency in the capital needed to carry the works through without interruption or,delaymight materially affect the estimates submitted to you in the prospectus as to the time and total of outlay required before the receipts anticipated could be earned, and so I endanger the security of the original shareholders."

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must mall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a. guarantee of good faith.

Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday.

Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one.

We cannot under ordinary circuinstance§,undertake return themanuscripts which are not used.

The Grappenhall Day School Manage- ment Committee. Sir,—l looked with some interest in last Saturday's issue, for .an explanation from Mr. W. H. Bradford in answer to Mr. S. Jenkins' letter on the above subject, and was astonished to find no reply. If the matter is as A Mr. Jenkins says and lam not aware that there is the v slightest reason to doubt it, then it was a course mean in the extreme and altogetherinnworthy of one bearing the name of Christian. The least Mr. Bradford could have done, if his name had been proposed suddenly and unawares to himself, was at once to explain what . his fellow Nonconformists had done, and leave the Council to refuse their nominee. Mr. Bradford owes an apology to the Nonconformists and especially to Mr. Jenkins, and as an honourable man he ought to with- draw from the position as he has not the confidence of his fellow-Nonconformists and therefore cannot repre- sent them. Besides,already he holds several offices,being a member of the Board of Guardians, the Sanitary Authority and the Parish Council. Why should he be so ready to accept another position? I have noticed a. tendency on the part of Parish Councils when choosing a Nonconformist representative to select the mildesb most insipid,and least robust of their kind. They do this as they suppose to avoid friction, but in doing so, they do an injustice to Nonconformists and cause more fric- tion and dissatisfaction. In any case such authorities would do well to appoint the men duly recommended by the Nonconformists themselves, as peaceful working can never be effected in this or any other parish, by ignoring the man whom the Nonconformists select as their representative and so adding insult to the injury caused by this most unjust act. lam yours &c., "HONOUR BRIGHT."

WALKING COMPETITIONS.

WALK BETWEEM WARRINGTON .I.:SD ECCLES. An unusual large crowd assembled oplaosite the Cross Keys, Buttermarket-street on Saturday afternooa to witness the arrival of the competitors in the walk from Eccles to Warrington and back. 'Warrington was the half way house, as it were, and as the com- petitors turned -round at the Cross Keys to perform the second half of the journey, the onlookers were given an opportunity of picking out the most likely winners. The first to arrive at Warrington was T. Platt, of Woolston, with Owen Davies of High. Legh close upon his heels. Owen Davies was then going strongly and therelwere many in Warrington who pre- dicted that he would be the winner. This turned out to be the case, and he won by about a minute. J. Isherwood and F. Mea- cock, both of Warrington, were second and third respectively, and they are to be congratulated on their performance. The competition was organised by the Eccles harriers. About £4O was offered in prizes. The walk resulted as follows :-1, Owen Davies, High Legh, 4hrs. 48mins. 12sec.; 2, J. Isherwood, Warring- ton, 41irs. 49mins. 20sec. ; 3, F. PEN KETH.

ANOTH NIL 17 I E.—Great exeitement was caused on Saturday morning last, bya fire which had broken out in a stable and loft belonging to Mr. Millington, Heath- road. The fire was first perceived by Mr. Peter Board- man at 4 tun., and he at once gave the alarm. A large number of the residents in the road, were quickly roused, and an amateur fire brigade quickly formed. The earliness of the hour prevented the Warrington Brigade being telephoned for, but by the aid of ladders and buckets the fire was eventually got under. It was fortunate the outbreak was discovered so soon,but as it was the roof was quite destroyed, and the contents of the loft ruined. The origin of the fire is unknown, and the damage is said to be covered by insurance. WKSLF.YAN CHAPEL—TR connection with the above, a special feature on Sunday night last, was the repeti. tion of the sacred Cantata 1` The New Jerusalem" which was rendered on the recent Anniversary Sunday. The performance„ of this piece was by request, and it seems a pity that the labour of the practising &c., should be given for one performance only. The excellence of the interpretation, warrants several repetitions, and it is hoped that the piece may yet again be given at a later period. Mr. James Dodds made a brief but telling address. The collection was in aid of the Sunday School funds, and amounted to VI.

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly oeneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. i Now the great need of the age s some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the prevalent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adapted to the needs of the mer- .chantoand the workman, the professional man, and he who 'wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the. :student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, sucha preparation a contains no injurious ingredients, and which May be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection ,of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed he an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, the Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has faifed to give rerief, and we may say further, that it has proved permanently beneficial, when other pre- parations at best only gave temporary relief. It is -strongly recommended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache, Pains in the Side Giddiness, Loss of Appe- tite, also for Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleep- lessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits and all kinds of Weakness. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A CARTER'S BAIBEZZLEMENT.

At Warrington Borough Police Court, on Tuesday before Alderman Sutton and other magistrates, Robert Blanchard, Bostock-street, was committed to prison for two months on charges of embezzling two sums of 18.9 d. and one or 10-Ad, on the 11th, :20th and 21st inst respec- tively, moneys received by him on behalf othiS master, Mr. -Joshua Pickstqn, coal dealer, 89, Liverpool-road. He pleaded guilty.—Mr. Steel prosecuted, and said that prisoner went out delivering coal from house to house. In some cases he received payment on delivery, and in ,others the coal was paid for at the end of the week. As Mr. Pickston had business at St. Helens,his wife managed, the business at Warrington. Each night the prisoner brought in his account to Mrs. Pickston to settler with the money he had drawn. Although they

ad only brought threg small cases against the pilsonei, the total defalcations amounted to about £lO. Mr. Steel describing how the prisoner obtained the money, said instead of paying the amount to Mrs. Pickston,he said the customer had not paid. Mrs. Pickston made out bills against the people prisoner said had not paid, and they came and denied that they owed lay money, stating that they had paid Blanchard at the time. Pri- soner was discharged by Mrs. Pickston. on Saturday, and arrested on a warrant.—The Chief Constable said prisoner had previously had 14 days for stealing a jacket and 12 strokes of the birch rod for stealing a shirt.

"BLACK AND BLITE.".

Before Mr. Birchall and other magistrates, at the Borough Police Court, on Saturday, John Farrington was charged under a warrant with assaulting his wife Mary Farrington, on July 23rd.—Complainant said that her husband came to her in the yard and struck her twice on the head, knocking her to the ground. He then tried to drag her into the house. On Wednesday evening he also assaulted her, and her arm was "black and blue " through his violence. She did not pick up a cobble stone to throw at him.—By the defendant: She did not pickup a knife to stab him with,nor did she pick up a'big cobble stone.- -The Police Matron deposed to having examined the complainant's body, which had been badly bruised. What she had observed must have been the result of great violence.—At this point the complain Ant bared her arms to the inspection of the Bench to show the ill-usage she had received.—Dr. Fox examined the woman's arms, one of which was in a shocking condi- tion.—The defendant accused his wife of striking him on the head with a poker and a flower pot. He said he had never struck her with his fist, but he had held her arms tightly—he might have been rough—to prevent her doing him an injury. He called one of his sons to speak in' his favour, and after a great deal of reluct- ance, the son admitted, in answer to a question from the Chief Constable, that "he had seen his father strike his mother."—The defendant was convicted of an aggra- vated assault, and fined 255. including costs.—The defendant was then served with a summons requiring him to show cause why a separation order should not be made against him.—The order was granted, and the magistrates ordered Farrington to pay ss. a week towards his wife's maintenance. SAD STORY OF A WLFE'S DRUNKENNESS.

It was a sad story told to the B3rough magistrates yesterday during the haring of an application by Robert Holbrook, of 47, Watkin-street, for a separation .order on the ground of his wife, Elizabeth being an habitual drunkard..--The applicant stated that for the last twelve months his wife had been . out of the house drinking and living a loose life. 'The care of his children had been left to his mother. His wife came into the house during his absence and took things away and pawned them. She spent the money he had received in drink. He had also found her consorting with other men.—lnspector Hackett said he had made several attempts to get the woman to lead a better life but it was all in vain. Since February last she had been going from bad to worse, and drinking ,continually. He thought it would be the best thing for the children, if a separation order were made and the care orthe children entrusted to the husband and his mother.—Superintendent Monaghan gave tile applicant a good character but was unable to say the same for the wo nan.—The magistrates asked her if she. would go to a home for six months, but she refused.— Inspe3tor Hackett said he and Vr. Taylor had both tried to induce her to go to a home but she would not g.—The magistrates granted a separation order but made no order as to maintenance. The defendant said she could get her own living by cutting.

DISGRACEFUL SCENE IN A TRAMCAR. Thoms Powell, of 39, Elizabeth-street,was summoned at the Warrington Police Court, yesterday on the double'charge of enterinq an electric car belonging to the Warrington Corporation whilst in a state of intoxi- cation, and also with interfering with the comfort of passengers.—Mr. Stanley Wilson (deputy Town Clerk) prosecuted,and stated that defendant was a puddler,and not only entered the car in a state of intoxication, but committed an assault on a passenger of a very cowardly nature. On Wednesday, the 22nd inst, as the ten o'clock car to Sankey Bridges arrived at the Post Office, a man named John Moore, of Booth-street, Sankey Bridges, got into the car. As soon as he had taken his seat he was followed by the defendant, who got in front of him and struck him a teriffic blow in the face with a stone which he held in his hand. Moore suffered considerably from the injuries to his nose and mouth, and it was with some difficulty that the conductor was able to hold the defendant until the arrival of a constable. The conductor could not find a constableat the Post Office and proceeded on the car to Sankey Bridges until he met P.C. Woods. A serious aspect of the case was that the defendant almost hita child which was in a woman's arms at the time. There were thirteen ladies in the car and they were much alarmed at what took place. He ventured to say the case was one of the worst he had ever brought before the Bench. It appeared that the two plen had met previously in a public house arid some disphte had arisen. There was not the slightest excuse for the de- fendant following Moore into the car and attacking him in the way mentioned. There was no necessity for the defendant to enter the car at all.— John Moore gave evidence as to being assaulted by the defendant.- They had previously been in the Wooipack . and they had an argument. The defendant followed him into the car and struck him with the stone. He lost a lot of blood and next day he could not go to his work.—Conductor Lythgoe also gave evidence bearing out Mr. Wilson's statement.—The defendant stated that Moore gave him two black eyes.—Asked why he assaulted Moore with a stone in his hand, defendant said Moore was "too big for him."—The magistrates fined the defendant 10s. including costs on each charge —2os. in all.

THE TRANSPORTER BRIDGE. Approaching Completion.

The sixth ordinary general meeting of the sharehold- ers of the Widnes 'and Runcorn Bridge' Company was hold on Tuesday at the offices of the secretary, No. 6, Castle-street, Liverpool. in the absence of the Chair- man (Sir John Brunner, Bart. M.P.), who had gone away for a short rest, the Deputy-'chairman (Sir Henry Seton-Karr, M.P.),presided, supported by the following directors :—Messrs. H. Wade Deacon, Holbrook Gaskell, jun., Samuel Owen, Samuel Quinn, and R. H. Posnett. There were also present Mr. Alsop and Mr. Oppenheim (solicitors), Mr. F. H. Stables (secretary), Mr. John J. Webster, and Mr. John T. Wood (engineers). The Chairman in moving the adoption of the report and accounts, which were taken as read, apologized for the absence of Sir John Brunner, Bart. Hesaid that he might state, on the authority of their engineers (Messrs. 3. Webster and J. T. Wood), who were present, that their bridge was making quite satisfactory progess. The towers on each side had been erected to about 160, foet, which was nearly theif total height. The masonry and anchorages were nearly completed,and the_wires,wit'l the cables, which were an important part, had been satis- factorily tested. The ropes were now in course of con- stniction, and a sample finished was tested at Birken- head last week with satisfactory results. Another im- portant point was that they had made a contract with Messrs Mather and Platt (Manchester) for the electric installation and equipment, including engine and dynamos to drive the car, and these were being made in duplicate, so that in the event of a break-down the traffic would not be suspended. Messrs. Mather and Platt had undertaken to finish their contract in six months. He hoped that the Bridge would be completed, before the next half yearly meeting. (Applause,) Mr. H. Wade Deacon, in s3conding the motion, said the bridge was expected to be completed by the end of this year or early in 1904. The early completion of the contracts demanded more money, and the direc- tors had just resolved to make another call of £2 per share, to be followed by a final call of £1 in November. They would then be in a position to pay for all their contracts, and the question bf debentures would be con- sidered later. He trusted that at their next meeting the bridge would be both open and earning money. (Applause.) The report and accounts were adopted unanimously. A vote of thanks was tendered to the chairman, and the suggestion of a shareholder that the Prince of Wales, or failing H.R.H.. then some other member of the Royal Family or other important personage, should be invited to ceremonially declare the bridge open, was received with enthusiasm.

The whole of the capital of, the company (£100,009) has been subscribed, £70,000 being called up. Only £5OO of the calls are in arrear. The amount expended to date of report was £56,001, there being .£13,700 balance in the bank.

FELL IN THE STREET.

A strange story comes from Bury, Lancashire, every detail of which is confirmed most absolutely. Mrs. W. Riley, who lives at 32, Ingham-street, Bury, had been ill for four years. Her trouble seemed to have its com- mencement in her Kidneys, for she had severe pains in her back which made it impossible for her to stand erect.

Another strange feature of her case was a giddiness which increased till at last she would fall down in the street, • being unable to stand when these giddy fits came on.

Her cure was as interesting as any that has ever been reported. She heard of Dodd's Kidney Pills, a remedy said to be a perfect cure for diseases of the Kidneys, and began by tryina one box. It is safe to say that as a remedy for all Kidney Complaints no other medicine has ever accomplished the results that Dodd's Kidney Pills have done.
