A FEAT OF DIGESTION.

We have frequently chronicled.'cases of perverted appe tite, but none so extraordinary as the one just reported by Dr. Mounier to the French Academy of Medicine. A young man was admitted to the St. Joseph's Hospital with epilepsy, Three weeks later the house surgeon dis- covered signs of a hard substance in the man's stomach. An operation led to the discovery that he had swallowed the greater portion of a table service ! Tn all, twenty- five objects were found, including eight coffee-spoons, the back of a fork with three prongs, the handle of a fork, two long nails, a needle, one knife blade, and a tooth from a comb. He had been thus indulging his haulware-appptite for about six months; and marvel- lous to relate, has completely recovered.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 1, 1903.

NOTES ON GIJAMES AND AT3IETICS.

Cricket.

The Earlestown men proved themselves worthy opponents of Warrington on Saturday when they played a drawn game at Earlestown. Warrington batted first and compiled the repectable total of 165, of which no fewer than 69 came from the bat of the captain, J. Bibby, who played a fine innings. Earles- town replied with 145 for 5 wickets, so that they can claim to have had by no means the worst of the struggle, which was -watched by something like 2,000 people.

Warrington were first represented by-Lynes and P. B. Hawtin, who were opposed by Howarth and Hatton. Runs came freely from the commencement, the professional driving with great power, whilst Hawtin effected some pretty strokes past cover. At 22 a double disaster befel the visitors, for Lynes touched i a rising ball from Howarth,and Alderson brought off a one-handed catch in the slips. W.H. Podmore was I bowled by Howarth before he had opened his account, and J. W. Bibby became the associate ,of Hawtin. ‘The bowling was played with confidence and twice I Hawtin placed Hatton to the rails. At 41 Howarth broke through Hawtin's defence with a good ball, making way for Thewlis, who was destined to have only a brief stay, fur Howarth bowled him with only eight rnus added. T. Houghton joined his captain and the score rising steadily, Alderson took the ball , from Hatton at 66, and immediately met with success, IHoughton playing across at the second delivery an: had his leg-stump disturbed. J. A. Briggs was bowled in the same over, and six" wickets were (I,)wn for 66. E. E. Woods cut Alderson for three, and Bibby scor- ing freely off Howarth that bowler was shunted for ;Davies. At 79 the new bowler made a successful appeal for Woods against infringing r ule 24.

J. Thompson came next and was content to see his captain slo the major portion of the scoring. At 132 the long partnership was brought to a close, the War- rington•captain playing 'a ball-on to his off stick. It was a beautiful innings, its chief characteristic: being the variety of strokes introduced during its compila- tion. Beyond a hard chance at the wicket when he had made 41,the innings. was without blemish, and its worth cannot be overestimated, for the bowling and fielding of the locals was very keen. Bibby hit seven fours.

• Further trouble was in store for the locals, for on J. Callon joining Thompson, another stand was made. Thompson now hit with his customary vigour, and 22 runs were scored in ten minutes, when a misunder- standing cost Thompson his wicket. The departing batsman had made 23, and his stay With Bibby was an object lesson in defensive cricket. The last wicket added ten runs before Hampson was caught in the long field.

Eariestown were left with a little over two hours in which to get the runs, and consideiing the size of the ground it was not a very big task. A promising start was made by Marsh and Husband who both .scored freely from Lynes and J. W. Bibby. At 45 Husband was caught at point, and T. Miller who was acezded a cheer in recognition of his fine innings the previous week against Liverpool, joined Marsh.. Runs still continued to come at a rapid pace,and the bats- men wee much in front of the clock. Thewlis took the ball from Bibby at 02: and the change checked the batsmen. Both bowlers were backed up with excellent fielding and at 75 a fine ball froth. Thewlis sent Miller back.

Crowther joined Marsh, and runs only came at rare intervals. This partnership resulted in the addition of 20 rims when Marsh failed to get back in time and Lynes threw down the wicket from mid on. Marsh had played capital cricket for his 43. S. Henderson joinedWowther, and steady play was the order. The century was hoisted, and Bibby tried again a place of Thewlis, but meeting with rough punishment, the old professional again resumed, meeting with succe,,ss, getting Henderson caught at mid on. Seven runs later Crowther was smartly run out by Podmore,-and G. B. Leigh and Howarth. were associated. It was now a quarter to seven, and rain, which had threatened for some time, commenced to fall sharply, so that the players had to retire and the game was left drawn.

The Warrington second eleven made a:much better show against Ear!estown second team than their seniors. They had, of course, the advantage of . the ground at Arpley, but the victory was due to plucky play. Batting first on an excellent wicket, the visitors from Earlestown scored 124,the chief contributors being F. J. Reade (30), W. Morse (25), and T. Bartley (20). Bennett took five wickets for 27 runs, F. Burton three for 28, and E. Pierpoint two for thirty. The heme team- gradually overhauled the visitors' score, the winning hit being made a few minutes from time, the Warrington score standing at 136 for 5 wickets. Bromley batted finely for his 38 not out and he received valuable assistance from W. Bennett (23 not out) and J. Hallam (20). It cannot be said that Sankey Wire Mills Club has done itself justice this season. • •On Saturday, they had again to suffer defeat; this ti ft at the hands of irlam O.W.S. who were visitors to tie Wire Mills ground. The margin was a very small one, but the visitors were no doubt very well pleased with themselves and their perjormance. The bowlinglvas,a feature of the afternoon's play. • On the winning side Caldwell captured five wickets for 14 runs and Tutin five for 15. On the winning side, N. Cook took 2 wickets for 3 runs, R. Cook 4 for 7 runs and Doody 4 for 17. H. Cook scored the highest number of runs, his contri- bution being 13 out of 33. Irlam's top scorer was Green with 10, the entire score realising 41.

The C.L.C. Engineers cut up rather badly on their own ground on Satukday when the Latchford Baptists were the visitors.. Angling.

In view of the principal Assiciation angling con- tests, the time for which is fast approaching, I would remind members of the necessity of entering for the same as early as posiible, so that arrangements can be made for successfully carrying out the same, as there are likely to be large entri,es. I would respect- fully remind members, also, that it is necessary that all competitirs should be clear on the books before these events take place, as au member • in arreftr taking part in the contests is liable to disquali- fication, or forfeiture of any prize won. Members fishing the Bridgwater Canal near any of the Company's wharves, are strictly to follow any in- structions ,given them by the Company's servants. Disregard of these instructions may bring ,such mem- bers into conflict with the Fishery Board, and also with the committee of the Association. Reports reach me of the use of trimmers in the old river, Lymm, an:l I would draw members' attention to Rule 3l,any infringement of which may result in expulsion.

Turning to angling matters of the .past week, capital reports • come in of good catches of fish from the Bridgewater Canal, principally from the neigh- bourhood of Grappenhall, fair specimens also being captured—one of our junior members securing one Of just under I.lb. —at London Bridge. Preston Brook ead has fished somewhat nraagrely but between that point and Moare, several fair catches are recorded, notably by Messrs. W. Bibby, J. Foster, Hobson, and Hackney.

No repprt is to hand from Newbridge, and only a moderate one from the old River, Lymm. The River Gowy fished fairly well last week end, W. Hamblett having about the best catch. Chester was practically a blank, which has been attributed to the very high tides prevailing at that time.. The North Staffordshire Canal at Acton Bridge has improved somewhat, H. Thomason jun., recording a good catch, said to be in the teens of pounds. From Rossett, only moderate catches are reported.

The Victoria Angling Society fished their first con- test in the Bridgewater Canal at Thelwall, and although the catches were not large, a very keen con- test ensued for the prizes offered, Messrs Hamblett, I. Briere, W. Bennett, J. Griffiths, W. 'Bate, F. Jolly, running out the principal winners in the order named.

The Travellers' Rest Angling Society fished their first match last week-6nd at Vale Royal, and very good catches are recorded. The winners ran out as follows, Messrs. Lomas, 21b. 7oz. ; W. Whit- field, lib. 1 1 oz. ; J. 'Wrench, 11b. Goz. 12drms. ; J.

lib. 33z. 3drs. ; J. Hadlaud, 11b. 21oz. • J. (Gopyright). Crossley , 15oz. 3drs„ &c.. 'every competitor being well. pleased with the outing. AN IVORY PORTRAIT.

BY MAUI) BECKETT,

Author of "A Girton Girl," " Child• Eyes," "Eliza," "That Slip of a Girl, " "A Great Heiress,"

When my uncle Silas died he left me heir to his very considerable fortune. We had always been the best of friends. He was a merchant at Calcutta, and as soon as my school and college days were over I had come out to join him, and. I had been with him ever since.

The Latchford Excelsior Society fished their first annual match at Broken Cross. The party of 53 members and friends drove in conveyances, and 31 took part in the match, and after the usual lapse of time, the following were the principal winners. J. Atherton, 11b. 2oz. 91drs., T. Leigh, A. Mather, T. Bailey, W. Wood, J. H. Wood, J. Mortinier. The prizes, to the value of 16 10s. were 'distri- buted at the Roan Cow on the 27th, after which a convivial evening was spent. Messrs. S. Lee,J. Orford, W. Wood, J. Robinson, J. Fish, N. Edleston, W. Ditch- field and J. Wolfenden, contributed to tlie harmony. The latter also made an efficient Accompanist.

He was a jolly, sporting old man, fonder of, and pluckier at, big game shooting than anyone I've ever met, and he was always ready to enjoy a good joke. Indeed, I had grown to look upon him as being nearly as young as was myself—the idea that the yearn were treating him less kindly than they were treating me never for a moment entered my thoughtless head. Occasionally he would complain of a queer feeling in the region of his heart, and he was at times subject to fainting fits, but I shut my eyes to the fact that anything was 'idrong, and when at last he was taken seriously ill as a horrible shock to me.

about my family I knew very little. My parents both died when I was a youngster, and I had only a dim recollection of them. Of other uncles, and cousins and aunts I had none. Indeed, we had no relations. I think we—my uncle and I—were the most destitute two in the world, as far as family connections went. It had never been my uncle's habit to talk very much about the old days before he left England, nor about the few relatives or friends he had ever possessed. There was only dim third cousin of whom he would sometimes speak, after dinner, when the lamps were lighted in our comfortable bachelor bungalow and we were quite alone, puffing at our cigars in the still air, after the intense heat of the day; but I always noticed a suspicious moisture in his eyes and a quiver round his lips when he mentioned Janet Fortescue's name. From the little he told me, I gathered that they had once been sweethearts, but they had had a tiff and parted. Well, to go back to the day when Uncle Silas was taken ill. It was horrible to see his dear old face ashy pale, the kind eyes drawn with pain, and his tall, soldierly-looking form laid low, grappling with death. It seemed impossible to realise the end was so near.

The movement to promote an 'angling contest for the benefit of the District Nursing Association proceeds apace,an efficient committee having been appointed,and under favourable conditions a good round sum should be realized for this deserving objects The ree usse of the Bridgewater Canal has been obtained, so that non-licence holders may assist to swell the number of entries. Preliminary notices will be circulated in handbill form in the course of next week, details will be published later, and the sale of tickets announced in due course. It has been arranged that the committee shall meet on August 4th at the Shakespeare Inn, Pinner's Brow, and that the meet- ings shall be held weekly until the contest takes place. Anglers please note that entries will close on September 29th. The best thanks of the committee have been accorded the Worsley Canal Fishery Board, for the handsome concession of free use of the canal for this important event.

He lingered on for a few days, until one morn- ing, when the faint light of the summer dawn had just begun to steal through the blinds, as I sat by his bedside watching his laboured breathing, with a lonely feeling at my heart, he opened his eyes, and motioned to me.

"Well, uncle," I said, in the most cheery voice I could muster, "how do you feel? You have had a good long Sleep." He raised himself on his arm for a moment, and looked at me with a dull hopelessness in his eyes, then he fell back among the pillows again. "Dick," he gasped, "those rubies! I haven't told you—they are in the oak box in my bureau— and her photo—Janet's—find her, find her, Dick," and he grasped my arm as his breath failed him. "Give her the rubies—don't rest day or night until you find her—promise, swear it." With one last effort his lingers closed on ray arm.

, His last words had puzzled me sorely. What did he mean about the rubies? I had not had the faintest idea that he had any jewels in his posses- sion. Had he been rambling? I hated the thought of searching among his private papers. It was an unpleasant duty, and I put it off as long as I could. However, it had to be done, and so one night I set myself to the task. I took his keys and opened the bureau. There, sure enough, was the oak box, just as he had said. At first I thought none of the keys would fit, as one after another I tried them, but at last one of them slipped in and turned in the lock easily. As I raised the lid it seemed to contain nothing but two packets of letters, carefully tied up and sealed. The papers looked old and yellow and worn, as if they had been carried about in somebody's pocket for a long time. I lifted them out carefully and lovingly—it seemed a desecration to disturb them—those treasures of my uncle's. Underneath them lay a small blue velvet case, and next to it a larger one of leather. .First I took up the velvet one, and with a press of my thumb the lid flew up. Inside lay an ivory miniature, exquisitely painted. The face was beautiful, more beautiful than any that I had ever seen; but it was none of. your Reynolds' or Gainsborough beauties, with eyes round with sentimentality and the sweet smile of a delicate imbecility on the stupid little mouths. No; this wOman's was an intellectual type; the forehead was low and broad, the skin delicately, exquisitely "air and shell-like, a.nd great dreamy, soft gray eyes, shadowed by their long black lashes, looked back at me. The mouth was not a rosebud one by any means; there were resolute and determined curves about it, although the whole expression of the face was sad, alniost mournful. Her hair, piled high on her shapely head in the fashion of the early thirties, was a most glorious chestnut, with strong red lights in it. The picture was set in a little filagree gold frame, and across one corner of it in tiny brilliants was the word "Janet." This, then, was the far-off cousin my uncle had talked to me about, always with a quiver in his voice. I gazed at the picture for some minutes—the face fascinated me; certainly it had never been my luck to meet with a woman half so beautiful. My mind began to conjure up many and varied tales, and then, with a start, I remembered the other case. It was fairly large and the spring was strong; but when the lid flew open it revealed a necklace of the most exquisite rubies I had ever seen. They were very large—about the size of the top of a big thimble—their colour was perfect, and their setting unique; but, strange to say, one of them, almost the biggest, was missing. Ten thousand pities, I thought, as I gazed at them with wonderment. The lights from the candle flickered round them, mak- ing them flash and glint; they were absolutely dazzling in their splendour. How on earth had my uncle got hold of them? Why had he kept them t secret from me? What had they to do with Janet vnrtescue ? Why was one of them missing, and who ...d it? All these questions I asked myself in a dazed kind of way. I went over his last words to me again and again: "Janet, the rubies; don't rest 'day or night till you give them to her." Well, I had sworn to keep my promise, and I had better set to work, without loss of time, to unravel the mystery and dispose of my charge ; it was dis- tinctly a responsibility to have jewels of that value suddenly thrust upon you belonging to someone else, especially in my bachelor, wandering life— and henceforward it probably would be a wandering one. Still, my uncle had managed to keep them safely hidden, even from me, for all these years, and so probably I could do the same. One by one I took out the old' letters and read them. Evidently they had been sent to him before he left England. They were all written in the same fine, delicate-pointed Italian hand, and sweet, womanly little letters, signed "Janet." The address on them .was Beech Manor, Woodfield. Not one of them contained any mention of the rubies, and after I had gone thiough them all I was no better off than before.

And, although my uncle had left his affairs in idle greatest order possible, there was no word about the jewels. Why, in a matter of such serious im- portance, had he left no word of instruction, no hint as to his wishes, to me, except those few last disjointel words with his dying breath ? .It was a mystery, and I felt sorely perplexed. As I remarked before, when all was settled up, , _ „mid myself in possesiion of a very considerable' fortune, quite independent, of everything and everybody„ without a tie in the world. At first after my uncle's death my life went on much as it had done before; I had made no plans as yet; but, instead of growing accustomed to his empty chair, I came to miss his cheery presence more and more, and then came an irresistible long- ing for the old homeland again. It was twelve years or more since I had left England, and, although India is a pleasant enough place to live in—many a good time have I had out there—still toy soul longed for my native shore once again. My mind was soon made up. I settled up my affairs, disposed of my comfortable bungalow. I

Cycling.

Although the weather was rather gloomy last Saturday about 15 members •of the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Cycling Club journeyed to Frodsham,arriving about 1 o'clock. Some limbed up the hill, others going for a short walk. Tea was provided by Mr. Webb. The return journey was made about 8 o'clock, Sankey being reached at about 9.30. To-clay the run is to Pickmere, on Monday, Bank Holiday, to Southport.

Rugby Ronal

Subject to the approval of the County Union, the "A " team matches will, next season be played by teams of 12 aside, so that the experiment will have adequate trial. In fact most of the junior Competi- tions under the Northern Union have decided to play 12 aside, and county matches will probably he on the same lines.

The Warrington Club has not yet made any impor- tations of note but, as usual, the emissaries of the club are said to be on the track of several "good men " In the meantime Arthur Burgess has been .transferred to Leigh, for a monetary consideration. He was cer- thinly a rank failure last season, but he has played some good games for - Warrington. M ay-be lie will do better at Leigh. He has youth and enthusiasm on his side anyhow, andif the Mather-Laners can find him a head for use on the field of play he may turu out a success.

PEN KETH.

ANOTHER FlRE:—Great excitement was caused on Saturday Morning last, by a fire which had broken out in a stabltland loft belonging to Mr. Millington, Heath- road. The fire was first perceived by Mr. Peter Board- man at 4 a.m., and he at once gave the alarm. A large number of the residents in the road, were quickly roused, and an amateur fire brigade quickly formed. The earliness of the hour prevented the Warrington Brigade being telephoned for, but by the aid of ladders and buckets the fire was eventually got under. It was fortunate the outbreak was discovered so soon,but as it was the roof was quite destroyed, and the contents of the loft ruined. The origin of the fire is unknown, and the damage is.said to be dovered by insurance. TELL-TALE FINGER PR INTS.

Interesting evidence was given at Kingston-on- Thames on Wednesday in the case of an old offender named George Hall, charged with three burglaries at Surbiton. The marks of the first three figures of a right hand were left on the paint of a study window in one of the houses entered. The wood was cut out and the marks photographed and enlarged. The prints of Hall's correipooding fingers were then taken in Brixton Prison, and it was pointed out to the magistrates that there were no fewer than fpurteen distinct points of resemblance whereas the number usually relied on was three. Half was committed for trial at the Surrey 'Quarter. Sessions.

THE "FREE-TRADER."

The Free Trade Union has published a capital first number of a weekly journal through which it proposes to give accurate information on all points that arise in the course of the " big fight." It is full of relevant and well arranged facts, with a spirited " leader" by Sir William Harcourt. The " Free-trader" ought to serve as an excellent check on the loose statistics and rash generalisations with which the country is being littered. his to be circulated gratuitously through clubs and associations ; copies of it will be at the disposal of Free Trade candidates for distribution, and single copies are to be obtainable for the cost of postage from the offices of the Union at 8, Victoria-street, London, S.W.

:aid good-bye to my kitmatgar, behrer, and all tha rest of them, and in a very short time I landed myself and my baggage on board the P. and 0. steamer Turin, bound for England. It was a corn. fortable boat; the passengers racist of them, wer:: alarming people, many of the girls were pretty, all of them were jolly, and varied were the enjoy- able half-hours • I passed. Altogether my voyage was a thoroughly pleasant one to look back upon. When I arrived in London I felt strange after ray lazy, calm existence in the East, where no one ever hurried; here everyone was in a bustle and a hurry, jostling you about as if they hadn't a moment to breathe. However, I did not waste much time in looking up some of my old college chums. Many of them were married to pretty wives, and we had a good time all round. But those rubies, they lay heavy on my conscience. I could not forget or neglect my charge, and before I could turn my thoughts pleasurewards, I must use every effort in my power to find out something about them.

I had no one tb question, no relations to ask, nothing to go upon but the name of Woodfield. The little knowledge I had was so slight that it was worth absolutely nothing. I travelled down to Berkshire to the little village of Woodfield. Once the family of Fortescue had lived at Beech Manor —so much I learnt—but they had, by some means that nobody seemed to know, lost ua heir -brtune in one crash, and the estate, not beilig entailed, they had had to leave it, and it had passed into other hands.

Soon after this, it was believed, brother and sister—all who were left of the Fortescue family— had died within a few months of each other. Meagre indeed was this information to go upon. I had all the parish registers of marriages and deaths searched, but they told me nothing. I caused advertisements to be inserted in every London and country paper for news of Janet Fortescue or her heirs. Arid now another trouble haunted me; were they—she and her family— living in comparative poverty, while I had posses- sion of these priceless jewels? My lawyers were ceaseless in their efforts; indeed, I think, we left no stone unturned; but all our search proved fruitless ; no answer came to any of our advertise- ments. Janet and her family seemed to have dis- appeared from the face of the earth. The months passed on and grew into years, and I made up my mind that my search was hopeless. The rubies, though, were rarely, if ever, out of my mind; they caused me sleepless nights, and bad dreams. Often I would wake up, a cold perspira- tion all over me, in the conviction that they had disappeared, until in my heart of hearts I had begun to think of them as "those blesasa rubies." I had been in England now for three years, and I had settled down peaceably to an English country life. After my visit to the little Berkshire village a fancy had possessed me that, if by any chance Beech Manor were in the market, I would buy it. I had some trouble at first about it, but before long I was able to come to a satisfactory agreement with the owner, and it passed into my hands. It was a snug house, and a nice little estate, the shooting was fairly good, and the hunting excellent. I had spent the winter here, but it was past now —the April days were lengthening into May, and I felt it was about time to take up my abode in town. One sunny afternoon I wended my way to the club. I had an appointment with Charlie le Mesuriex ; we were to talk over some horses I wanted to buy. He hadn't turned up when I arrived, so I sat down, wrote off a few letters, and then, picking up the first book my hand came to, I flung myself into an armchair to wait for him. I looked at the book I had taken up—it was the "Academy Notes" —idly turning the pages. They didn't look a par- ticularly interesting collection, but, stay I—the leaf turned over quickly. Surely there was something familiar in the pose of that graceful head. I scanned the print eagerly. Yes lit must be—it was more than a passing likeness—the face was exactly like my miniature. Forgetting all about my appointment with Charlie le Mesurier, I dashed down the club-steps, jumped into a hansom, and was at Burlington House in much less time than it takes to tell. Room VIII. —there it was, and all doubt ceased, it was the portrait of Janet Fortescue or her double. Th, curly chestnut hair was piled up 'high on the beautiful head, in almost exactly the same way as in the little miniature, the eyes deep, dark, and softly gray, and the same, sad, thoughtful expression in them, and the mouth, with its determined lines, had still the same pathetic droop. But, then, Janet Fortescue must be a very old woman by this time, 1 reflected, and this undoubtedly was quite a recent painting. My fingers trembled with excitement as I found the number in the catalogue: "Portrait of a Lady," by John Jones. Wise information—l could see that for myself. "Idiot I" I muttered. Had I got a clue, and were all my fruitless efforts to meet with success at last?

The first thing to do was to find out where the artist lived, aid get what information I could out of him. I turned to the back of the book—Mr. John Jones, 9, St. John's-road, Putney. I told my cabby to drive there like lightning, At No. 9 I went up the steps and rang the hell. A stolid- looking maid-servant came to the door. In answer to my hurried question if her master was at home: "No, he was away in the country, and would not be back for feveral weeks." "Would she give me his address "it' She left me waiting for a moment, then emerged from one of the rooms with it written down on a slip of paper. I turned my steps back to the hotel, where I sat down and wrote to Mr.. Jones. I told him some of the story, and begged him by return of post to send me the name and address of the lady. Then all I could do was to wait for the answer with what patience I could. The picture occupied my thoughts; by night I dreamt of it, and through the day the face haunted me, and to be haunted is by no means a comfortable experience for any man. The next day came, and the next, but no answer to my letter, until a week had passed, and then I wrote again. For the fifth time I wended my way to Burling- ton House, and to Room VIII., straight up to the picture—my picture, as I had begun to think of it. I went and gazed at it as I had done many times before. Any passer-by, who took the trouble to notice, might have formed the opinion that I moon-struck or or daft. Certainly I resented every look and exclamation of admiration,--and they were many—which the lovely portrait called forth. One thing about the picture which had excited me more than all was that her fingers were innocent of rings, except that on the third finger of her left hand she wore what looked like an immense ruby. I stood looking at it—as usual, indulging in the wildest day-dreams, but as day-dreams are quite the most unsatisfactory things in this prosaic world, I knew I was a fool, and, giving myself a shake, I stepped back. As I did so I came in contact with a lady.

I was just turning away, after begging her pardon, when she raised her eyes to mine! Great Heavens It was the very same face as that in the picture, and she was a young woman of about five or six and twenty. A little smile crept round the corners of her pretty mouth as she stood looking at her own portrait. I. felt myself almost shaking with suppressed excitement. What was I to do? Dare I go up and speak to her, and ask her to listen to me? No! Probably she would walk away, and take no notice of me. I sat down on one of the lounges in the middle of the room, and tried to think. My brain felt in a muddled condition. Those rubies, I began to imagine, had affected it sadly. I was simply at my wits' end. - I watched my lady's graceful figure as she went from picture to picture. She was exquisitely dressed, and I noticed that everyone turned to look at her; then she went on to the next room, I following her at a respectful distance, and so we went on, she carefully examining almost every picture. Then she turned her attention to the sculpture. Would she ever finish ? However, my patience gained its reward when I saw her go through the turnstiles, down the stairs, and out into the courtyard, I quickly following her. Wonld she, call a hansom? No! With her stately grace—better than any queen—she walked out into Piccadilly and hailed—a Hammersmith omnibus. She got in, aid I jumped ih after her. Opposite her I had a good opportunity of studying her face. Beautiful! The artist had not done be, justice by any means. When we came to a certain long vied, with tall, gloomy-looking houses on either side, she stopped the omnibus and got out. I went on for a few yards and then jumped off, and hurried to the corner of the road, just in time to see her disap- pearing into a door some way down—it was No. 13. Then I wondered what I should do. There were a few shops at the top of the road; one of these entered.

"Can you tell me who lives at No. 13 ? " "Thirteen's a boarding-house," was the laconic answer from the greengrocer woman. "Is it a large boarding establishment, and who keeps_ it ? " "Mrs. Raymond is the lady as keeps it,and there aren't many people staying there, only two lady

District), held at the Q 40; Hough, 1,164; Killcross, 1,051 ; Clucas ,887. afternoon for the purpose; of discussing the comnensa - I tion question, Mr. T. J. Down, of Warrington, who presided, said that, as they were aware, magistrates in various parts of the country had for some time past, and especially at the last licensing Sessions, been refusing the renewals of licences in a wholesale and most arbitrary manner, and many benches had stated plainly and openly that it was their intention to deal still more drastically with the matter next year. That placed them in a vereaserious position as a trade. Many of them had already suffered most severely from the action 'of the licensing magistrates, and he was afraid they had no remedy for what had been done up to the present time. What they now wanted was protection for the future. Members of the Government, the House of Commons,and others had affirmed that whatever the strict letter of the law might be, they were morally entitled to the renewal of , their licences except iu cases where-they had been for- feited owing to misconduct, and that when refused for any other reason those interested should be duly com- pensated. The Government were practically pledged to bring in a Bill on the subject next session, and he noticed it was stated last Saturday apparently with authority, that Mr. Balfour would, before Parliament rose, announce that the Government intended early next session to bring in and pass a Bill providing for compensation to licence-holders whose licences had been withdrawn for no fault of their own. They would observe the words "compensation to licence-holders," but they must see that it went further than that. It was only right that the licence-holder , should have every consideration, but the vast majority 1 of licensees were tenants only of the houses they occu- pied, and there were t he interests of the owners to be taken into account and provided for. The loss of a lieence way a serious matter for a tenant, but it was still more serious for the owner of the property, as it often meant to him the loss of a large sum of money, in some cases amounting to thousands of pounds. Then again the Bill should be made retros- pective, so far as the ensuing licensing sessions were concerned at any rate. (Hear, hear.) This last con- dition was absolutely nOcessary, and they would have to do everything in their power to obtain an assurance from the Government that such a clause shourn be inserted in the bill. Unless that could be accomplished he greatly feared that next February would bring them fresh trouble and loss. The confiscation craze was in the air. It had spread like an epidemic ever the country, and although it might to some extent have exhausted itself, it was by no means dead, and it behoved them to be on the alert and to do all that they honourably could to protect and safeguard their interests. (Applause). He proposed the following resolution :- "That this Meeting representing all the interests of the Licensed Trade in North \Vales, Cheshire and Shrop- shire, expresses its deep regret that the speech of the Prime Minister to the Trade Deputation on March 18th has, so far, led to no practical result in theslirection of the passing of a Bill in favour .of equitable compensa- tion, and urges upon the Government, that the measure promised by the Lord Chancellor, and other members of the Government should be introduced at the earliest 'opportunity next session and passed into law, so as to secure the licences of well conducted houses from con - fiscation ; and, further, that such a measure shall be, in its operation, retrospective, se far as the Licensing Sessions of February, 1004, are concerned." The resolution was carried unanimously.]

RAILWAY DISASTER AT GLASGOW.

At St. Erioch's Stltion, Glasgow, the terminus of the Glasgow and South-Western Railway, on Monday morning a long and heavily-laden train conveying excursionists returning from the Isle of Man, via Ard- rossan, dashed into the terminal blocks of the platfcrm and was wrecked. Fifteen persons were killed and many others were seriously injured. The platforms of the station vary in length, and the driver of the train appears to have been looped on to a short platform and miscalculated his speed, which had apparently been arranged for a long platform, having no space in which to pull up. The train dashed into the stationary buffers, and the twoloremost carriages were telescoped with awful suddenness. The victims include a family of four. The engine driver and fireman bravely remained at their posts, and escaped injury. The former, who is in custody, frequently mutters: "Would to God I had also died."

LOCAL TRIBUTES TO THE LATE

POPE.

Reqiiiem Mass at St. Mary's,

Buttermarket-street

A large congregation assembled in St. Mary's Roman Catholic Church, 13uttermarket-street, on Tuesday morn- ing, when a solemn requiem mass was sung for the repose of the soul of the late Pope. Many townspeople assembled as a mark of respect. Dr. Mercgr was the celebrant, and the other priests in attendance were Father Hind (deacon) and Father Primavesi (sub- deacon.) The church pillars and parts of the altar had .been draped in black. The music was the plain chant requiem mass, and was very impressively rendered. Mr. T. Mather presided at the organ. The Rev. Father Wilson paid a fitting tribute to the worth of the deceased Pontiff. This is a solemn and 1 sorrowful occasion, he said, but it is not one for many words. Indeed not many are needed; your own beasts speak to you better than I can, and respond more eloquently to the emotions of the hour. Yet though it is difficult to speak befittingly of one who to us is above critic'stu and bey al adequate appreciation, it is hard to be entirely Tdent of one whom we revere so deeply and love so much. Nine days ago you received news that the Sovereign Pontiff or, as we love rather to style him, the Holy Father, had passed away, and that the long struggle that the world had watched with deep and sympathetic interest, was over. Catholics felt that they were sadly bereaved and orphaned, that they had lost their spiritual ruler and teacher, their most wise guide, their devoted father. Those who owned not his sway still owned that the world was poorer for his loss, and that a man of unique character and achievements had been withdrawn from the arena of the world. They mourned a man of extra- ordinary vitality, of untirin,, energy, of the rarest combination of endowments, of incomparable simplicity and purity, as well as persistency of purpose. We regard the Pope as the vicar of Christ—by Divine institution bishop of bishops—and every faithful Catholic owns him as his immediate superior to love and to obey. In him exists that bond of unity which holds and has held the Church of Christ together, though spread throughout the world and exposed through the centuries to the attack of every hostile force. There are those, of course. who cannot bring themselves to admit and acknowledge these proud claims, and we do not expect it, but they have not been slow to acknowledge Leo XIII.'s iclaim of worth upon their esteem and of goodness upon their affection. Many times I have been deeply gratified by the kind and respectful inquiries and expressions of sympathy received from many of our fellow townspeople during his illness, and upon his death, I am even more gratified to see here many to join us in honouring the dead. These are the kindly incidents which tend to lessen that friction and soften that antagonism which we all desire to see utterly extinguished, but which, perhaps, is at times inevitable where interests are supreme and diverse convictions deep and firm. It is difficult for me to speak of the deceased Pontiff. It is impossible to have taken even a superficial interest in the events of the last 25 years without having some image of Leo XIII. impressed upon one's mind and imagination. He is im- pressed upon my mind as a man who, having drunk deeply of all the fountains of knowledge—having mixed in the world and assimilated its most varied experi- ences—having learned what is in man from the search- ings of his own inmost heart—and received i n prayer and contemplation a light and wisdom more unerring than any of this world, having drawn from the sources of grace more than natural strength of character, was by Divine Providence taken and exalted above his fellows and put over the world, placed in its highest watch tower, to be its watchman and keeper. He was there, not for his own aggrandisement but for the world's good. Not a trace of self or selfishness is to be discovered in any word or act of his. To convince men that the world could not do without religion, that its heart-rending miseries were for lack of it, and that the Church, of which he was the head, was the only unerr- ing and authorized exponent of religion, and so to lead her members to be zealous and loyal, and to load those without into the fold, was his meat and drink, his very life. To this end from his high watch tower he warned, rebuked, encouraged. In this task there was not a subject of human interest, natural or supernatural, that he did not touch, and, touching, enlighten and adorn. Two features of this wonderful life- work strike us with great emphasis—the con- siderate tact and inexhaustible patience with which he dealt with difficulties and oppositions. To- wards our own country he has shown a gracious solici- tude and unfailing affection. An inexhaustible patience and strong persistency of purpose was surely a marked characteristic of the late Pontiff. Anot'ier, was his tenderness for the less fortunate in this world—the oppressed, the poor, the toiler. The greatest questions of the day—the social questions—received his closest attention, and were illuminated by his highest wisdom, directed by his wonderful prudence, softened by his great considerateness. Boldly he defined the ' rights of property, boldly also he preached its duties and responsibilities;iboldly he rebuked covetousness and oppression; but while he showed a tenderness for the hard-set toiler that the more powerful and wealthy needed not, he did not fail to impress upon him the peremptory obligations of honesty and industry. Ho has well earned the title of the Workman's Pope. To all " this breadth of mind and strength of character he added a singular personal charm and a rare refinement and culture. But we are here not simply to honour Pope Leo's memory, and to give outward expression of our reference and love. This we do in common with all the world: But we do more.—We remember—he above all would wish us to remember, that he was but a man, a frail man, subject to like passions as ourselves, liable to sin. Therefore we are here to offer up the awful sacrifice of atonement and with it our heart's prayer that the last remnants of imperfection may be purged away,and that he may be admitted to the rest he yearned for—the reward of his labours, and the blissful company of the Divine Master he served so long and faithfully. May he rest in peace and may perpetual light shine upon him.

===== 0002601_19030801_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
Reference at St. Alban's Church.

At St. Alban's Church, 13ewsey-street, on Sunday evening, reference was made to the late Pope. The Church and altar were draped in black, and the choir sang the following pieces :—lntroit, "Requiem reteryam" from the Gregorian Mass for the Dead; "0 rheritum passionis" (solo by Mr. J. Bate) ; " Jesu, Salrator mundi," after each mystery of the Rosary; the psalm. "De profundis clamavi" (during Benediction of the Sacrament), while at the end of the service the'orf_ranist, Mr. 3. Caldwell, played the blessed" Dead March in "Saul." •

41:J1E:3-Rev. Father Bernard Gibbons, in the course of an address, saia they were met to join in the universal prayer that was going up to the Throne of God for their late Holy Father, Pope Leo XIII. They must/ lift up their dyes from the little circle in which their lives were cast, and look to the great Catholic world Leyond --to those 250 million Catholic hearts—who mourned his death, and not only to his Catholic subjects, but to all the civilized nations of the world, who admired and respected him. From every quarter of the earth they find would expressions of the highe:;:; praise, as Pope Leo XIII. had outshone the glory of most of his predecessors. He had distinguished himself :.s a diplomatic statesman, a true pastor—and a.; an ascetic. As a statesman his relations with the Powers of Europe were well-known to those who had followed the history of our times. Solite of the difficulties he had to con- tend with were the Plan of Campaign in Ireland, the Kulturkampf in Germany, the educational difficulties in the United States. The wonderful success of enter- prisEs such as these had gained for Leo XIII. the reputation of being the greatest diplomatist of our age. In the dispute between Spain and the Urited States, he was chosen as'arbitrator. In fine the whole world regarded Leo as a great man himself and asa ruler of men. They loved to look on him as a ruler of souls, as a pastor and shepherd of the flock of Christ. As chief Bishop he had made him- self far more distinguished. Leo XIII. had been constantly at work. His published writings had now reached to 20 large volumes. Their subject matter con- cerned the most important things that mankind had to consider here below. Deeper still was their admiration of him as an ascetic, as a man full of virtue. His simplicity of life, his patient fortitude during the long years of imprisonment in the Vatican, his dignified re- signation, his deep humility in the highest position this world can offer, his sincere piety evident in every page of his writings, and his affection for the Mother of God, these things deepened more than anything else their res- pect and admiration for him, for they were evidence of a saintly life—a life which all the riches,intellect and honours of the world were powerless to produce. The death of Leo had already had its effect upon the world. When a great man died,the eyes of the whole world were turned upon his life. People were in- fluenced by it. Men if they were thinkers would now look into the late Pope's life. They would easily discover its characteristic trait. Leo's most prominent idea was that of the Divine authority of. the Catholic Church ; and that if men would only recognize it and obey the Church it would cure the many social miseries of our day. This was the theme of most of his encyclicals. If men would read them theywould be influenced by them. They wonld be brought nearer t 3 the Church of God with its world- wide dominion, its.wonderful organization; they would see clearer the reasonableness of the truths it set forth and the justice of its claims. The life that was ended must have its effect upon us. It was a striking example of strenuous work—of work that had but one object before it, which was the glory of God and the spread of His earthly kingdom.

It is believed that the accident was caused by the brake being appl:od so suddenly as to break the wheel. THE EDUCATION ACT AT WORK,

AN INTERVIEW WITH THE REV. THOMAS LAW.

There are two sides to every great movement —the one which the public sees, the imposing demonstrations, the battle of, words, the heated campaign, and the other which is known but to a few, the quiet organising, the steady collecting of facts the delicate negotiating and attention to the thousand and one details that continually crop up. The Education Campaign in a striking way contains these two sides, and the Rev. Thomas Law, the secretary of the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches, is familiar with them both. Meet- ings have been arranged by the thousand, from the great St. James's Hall meeting, which initiated the campaign, and similar demonstra- tions, to the humble gathering in the obscure village. Millions of tracts, books, etc:, have been distributed. Speakers have been pro- vided for places far and near. The Act is now in operation, but that does not mean the cessa- tion of the campaign, and one of the, perhaps, most important features of the work is the collecting of facts in relation to the administra- tion of the Act all over the country, and the taking of action when it is necessary, in order to conserve as much of the 'rights of Free Churchmen as remain. Mr. Law was good enough to grant me, writes our representative, a short interview on this phase of the work. "How do you obtain your facts as to the working of the Act?" I asked. - "In this matter," replied Mr. Law, "we have the invaluable assistance of the secretaries of our 800 Councils all over the country, and forty-five County Federations of Councils. Schedules, dealing with such points as the denominational and political composi- tion of the local Education Authorities and the Education Committees, proportions of boards of manager, denominations of head-teachers, etc., were prepared, and our secretaries have crone to considerable trouble to answer the questions we have put. We are just now send- ing out further schedules dealing with one or two details it is desirable to make quite clear. Returns are now, pouring in to our offices at the Memorial Hall, and are being collated. Intricate cases—and comparatively simple cases, too—have often required a great.deal of correspondence, and special inquiries. We have spared no efforts to ascertain the exact facts for the purpose of placing them before the country. Wb must acknowledge the devo- tion, loyalty, and perseverance of our local secretaries in gathering this information. Simple, as it often seems, it requires careful thought and attention to gather the required information." "Then how do you find that the Education Act is being worked?"

"In many cases, I am sorry to say in. a very high-handed fashion. Our fears have been more than realised, and dangers that were not suspected when the Bill was before Parliament have now been revealed. Of course, when our educational machinery wat placed in the hands of authorities elected for quite another issue, and with no regard to denominational quali- fications, it was inevitable that in many cases, injustice would be done to Free Churchmen.. This evil often has been made worse by co- option. In Liverpool, for instance, the City Council actually elected a majority of Free Churchmen, but by a wide exercise of the co-option clause, the Anglicans have gained the ascendency on the Education Committee. Again, at Newport, Isle of Wight, among the Council .representatives—the men responsible to. the ratepayers—the Nonconformists were only in a minority of two, but by using the co-option clause wholly for their advantage the Anglicans placed themselves in a majority of six. Quite apart from co-option, in many cases the proportion of Free Churchmen on Education Committees is lamentably small. In the East Riding 'of Yorkshire, for instance, out of a membership of. thirty, the Free Churches are represented bY only five of their faith as against seventeen Anglicans. "Do you find, Mr. Law, that Free Church- men are appointed representatives of the public on the denominational schools ? " "I find it very rare indeed that the two managers which the local authorities are able to appoint on the Anglican schools are Free Churchmen. During the passage of the Bill thfough Parliament, much was said of the futility of two Nonconformist representatives out of a board of six, but really it is seldom that Nonconformists get even the two. Some Education Committees appoint their two managers of a differ9nt denomination to that of the school, but they are not many. What popular control is guaranteed, for instance, by the condition of things such as obtains in the Holland Division of Lincolnshire? There, forty-two clergymen and one Catholic priest have been appointed as managers of the Voluntary schools. The Council schools are being captured in the same way; in the same divison thirteen clergymen being appointed as managers as against two Free Church minis- ters. At Worthing, too, the Education Com- mittee refused to allow more than three Non- conformist managers out of a total of forty- two." "Do Nonconformists get fair play in the scholastic profession?" " Gomparatively few of the higher positions in the scholastic world," replied Mr. Law em- phatically, "are in the hands of Noncon- formists, and in most of the returns that have reached our office, Free Churchmen form an insignificant minority on the teaching staff. At Wolverhampton, for example, out of twenty-five head-teachers in the provided schools, twenty-one are Anglicans, while in the denominational schools, out of forty-six head-teachers, thirty-three are Anglicans, ten are Roman Catholics, and only three Non- conformists; so that in the public schools of Wolverhampton there are only seven head- teachers who are Free Churchmen. The new Act gives mo promise of altering this state of affairs ; advertisements for teachers 'must be Churchmen' still appear in large numbers in the scholastic papers. "In many instances the change in the Edu- cational Authority has been used to the ad- vantage of the teachers in Voluntary schools, their salaries being increased in view of the schools being thrown on to the rates. A Wolverhampton head-teacher had his salary increased from £225 to £249 per annum, just prior to the change. According to the stan- dard of the School Board of the same place, he would only be entitled to £lBO, At Here- ford, too, it was shown that increases had been made only a short time before the trans- fer, and the Hereford Education Committee at ,first demurred, and the signing of the cheques for teachers' salaries was deferred, but ultimately they were paid. A similar state of affairs was revealed at Shrewsbury, a headmistress' salary being raised £lO, two teachers £lO and £2O respectively, and an infants' mistress £2O. The increase is dated a few days before the placing of the schools on to the rates."

"What do you intend to do with the -facts when complete ? " "In addition to the wide publicity we pro- pose to give them through the Press all over the country, as soon as we can exactly ascer- tain the, composition of the numerous Educa- tion Authorities all over the country, we pro- pose to publish in book form the statistics tabulated so that the Npnconformist position nun hA RAATI at a glance. Of ronrss. ito, portance of this can be understood, but may say the labour involved can only be imagined. We are striving to get all the facts so as to place before the country exactly how the Education Act is working. Where it is necessary we take action on cases as they come in; our Education Legal Sub-com- mittee, consisting of Mr. Lloyd George, M.P., Dr. Clifford, Mr. E. C. Rawlings, Mr. J. Bam- fami Slack f the legal adviser of the National

THE GENERAL ELECTION.

"May I ask, Mr. Law, if the National Free Church Council will take any part in the next General Election?"

"Already we are in communication with numbers of prominent Free Churchmen, urg- ing them to stand as candidates. Nothing but the gravest issues would have called some of them forth, but they feel it their duty at this crisis to place their services in the hands of the country." _

"But, Mr. Law, will not this be regarded as party politics P" _

' 'Not at all. We are in the midst of a great religious struggle which affects the whole life of Nonconformity, therefore we are bound to make it a vital question at the election. The coming struggle will be the greatest fight in which Nonconformists have been engaged since the days of Cromwell, and we shall thoroughly organise in order to be ready when the time comes. We are already preparing our plan of campaign; the organisation which the National Free Church Council has built up all over the country, and which is on the whole splendidly efficient, will be exerted to the uttermost to win this battle. The political leaders who are in opposition to the Act fully realise that the National Council will be a most important factor in the next General Election, as well as all municipal and other elections. It is now of the greatest import- ance that Free Churchmen should see that their names and those of their friends are on the register of electors. Local councils, too, should carefully watch the selecting of candi- dates in their constituencies. We must all work together in the confidence of victory."

A POACHER DROWNED AT RIXTON.

The story of the way in which a Salford poacher named William Wilson met his death,was told in the Coroner's court at Salford on Monday afternoon. Wilson was a boatman by occupation. He went with another boatman named Green on a flat down the Mersey to Rixton, near Warrington, and landedat a place where they had previously set some nets. They found a couple of rabbits in the nets, and then Wilson called out to his companion that someone was coming. Four men walked towards them and, according to Green's state- ment, violence was used. Green alleged that he was struck on the forehead with a stick, was kicked several times, and after running a hundred yards fell in a dazed condition. He heard Wilson shout as if in pain, but did not see him struck at all.

This statement was rebutted by the police and keepers, who constituted the party encountered by the poachers. The evidence of Constable Clarke was to the effect that three employs on the estate solicited his assistance as they were expecting poachers. They came upon three men, and blows were struck. Then the men turned and ran. The policeman followed one of the men who had struck him onthe hand, andran him across a field. The man was turning to go down an embankment towards the Mersey, when he caught his foot in a rabbit net and fell head foremost down the embankment. He rolled to the bottom got up, and started running along the river bank. When witness was about two yards from him the man took one step down the bank, jumped into the water, and swam towards-the other side. He heard him cough once or twice. That was the last seen of him alive, and later his dead body was recovered from the water.

The Coroner directed the jury to dismiss from their minds the idea that the man had been injured before getting into the water, and to bring in a verdict of Accidental drowning." The jury, however, returned a verdict of" Found drowned."

A remarkable divorce action brought by Mr. Demetrius Sophocles Constantinidi on the ground that his wife's bigamy with a doctor named Lance resulted in the jury assessing the damages at £25,000—a record for the court. The jury found that the petitioner had also been guilty of misconduct. Argument on a point of law was re- served. Mrs. Constantinidi is a member of the Ralli family, who have a great banking business in London, Manchester, and Liverpool. To whom do Canada and virtually one free port. Australia sell their food-stuff? Surely it goes to Great Britain. I have in recent articles dealt with Canada. Increase her production of food-stuff and she will com- pete more keenly with the States. But keep this one very important fact in mind, and believe me when I state it, Canada is but another North America in her immensity and in her resources, and she will in spite of all attempts to make her a member of the happy family , remain a stubborn child and pursue her own course Canada and the other Colonies will sell to Britain al' they have to sell and no more, Zollverein or no Zollverein. But Mr. Chamberlain's idea of Protection carries with it the charmed word of " reciprocity." If we, the British, make special rates, tariffs, with the Colonies, why, the same policy will be meted out' to us. Here comae in the sweet simplicity. Theßrifish workmen will allow Canadian food-stuff to come in free, but will place a tax on that from the States. Up to the margin of the protective tariff the Canadian farmer will go in his prices as near as possible. If the American farmer gets any grain into Liverpool it will be with a tax tagged on, so the British workman's cheap loaf vill have two rats nibbling at itHe must mind or it will be only a small crust he will have left for himsdlf. But says this "reciprocity scheme" in return for this we shall find markets, or retain markets in wiiidir to dispose of our products, industrial, mechanical and so forth. In addition,we shall create a fund out of which we can pay out pensions to the worn-out workers. What a gamble with the food of the people this is. If asystem of pen- sions is to be forthcoming it must in no. sense depend for its source upon a taxation of food-stuffs, or upon the imperial idea. Imperialism, or big Englandism has but a rotten stick to lean upon, that stickis sentiment.: As John Bright said, "Force is no remedy," and neither the mother nor yet her children can force their business I men to adopt usubusinesslike methods. What Mr. Chamberlain and his supporters areafter,is to secure the material prosperity of Great 'Britain. They are of opinion that Great Britain and her Colonies could, if welded together into one compact body defy. the world. Two features come out of this position, viz., defiance, or defence in the event of war, and in- creasing prosperity on trade lines. The first is a mere figment of the brainsmd events in the South African war proved it, and the second has been dealt With. The In- dependence of Great Britain and her Colonies must be maintained, each must go its own way, and nothing of an artificial character must be attempted. The Colonies are a dead weight, a burden not to be much longer carried, as in the individual, so in the =Altitude self

will rule.

TREE TRADE ZONES IN ITALY.

HAROLD COX, secretary of the Cobden Club,writes:---- There is a movement now in progress in Italy which has an important bearing 'upon the controversy raised by Mr. Chamberlain. At a meeting of the Union of Italian Chambers of Commerce held in Rome ot-i..lune 28th and 29th, resolutions were passed demanding the immediate establishment of Free Trade Zones in the .chief maritime cities. The following are the principal regulations pro- posed:- 1. All goods may freely enter these zones with. our paying any customs duty, and may equally be sent out free of duty if destined for ekport. 2.. The manufacturing and other handling goods in these zones will be exempt from all customs formalities and duties. 3. The necessary buildings of every kind will 'be allowed within the zones. 4. Food and drink consumed witian the zenes

MR. C. E. PARKER SUPPLIES TWELVE REASONS IN FAVOUR .OF FREE TRADE.

Mr. PARKER has forwarded the following treasons :- 1. Because Protection always has had, and ever wilt have,the effwt of increasing bola the cost of living,and that of manufacture. 2. Because this increased cost presses hardest on those who are least able to bear it, especially if foed- stuffs be taxed. S. Because all increases in price-lessen the spending power of the'people,atti so reduce the amount available for theaustaining of existing industries,and the develop- ment of new ones. 4. Because Protection by annihilating competition, makes the way easy for 'trusts -and combinations to monocolize the agencies of.production nad fix theirown prices for the necessaries, and conveniences of life. 5. Because every industry which rests upon Protec- tion, takes capitol and labour out of 'employments naturally remunerative, andencouragea them to engage in employments which are artificially remunerative at the expense of the consumers, thus causing a diminution of wealth and a reductionin the wage fund. G. Because by maintaining the full right of our people to buy from every country in the world we secure all the bargains. Eneand buys -better than any other country. 7. Because by Free Trade we bind the countries of the world inthe bonds of mntuallinterest, Which factor makes for peace and interaagonal good. 8. Because by the free admission of all the,besttpro- ducts of-the brain and skin of the workers in all lands, our consumers are benefited and our manufacturers are subject t o healthy stimulus which frequently results in their competing with and rivalling the imported pro- 1 duct. 9. Because as Sir Robert Peel said "We can best I fight foreign tariffs by free imports." 10. Because, while the wonderful prosperity of this i country Since Free Trade was introduced is doubtless in 1 part due to the increased facilities of • transit and lessened cost of conveyance, it is 'Free Trade that i enables us to take full advantage of, and put to the best use, these benefits. 11. Because Free Trade benefits all, for we are all 1 consumers ;Protection only benefits-a liniited. number 1 of producers, and that,at the cost of the unprotected I producers, and society generally. Protection is class ,I legislation, and like all such, benefits the few at the ' expense of the many. 3.2. Because if Protection is introduced it will bring I into state affairs a disastrous rivalry of tradeinterests, which will take up the time and energy of a Parlia- ment already overburdened with the cares of a great

THE DEAR LOAF.

The Examiner, Saturday August 1, 1903.

point be turned towards the Treaties of Great Britain as with other countries, bearing upon this question. But before I would support Protection even in this guise, I would go through the iron and wire mills, and

know I should find out one or two important secrets Leave out the business feature, and I ask can any of my readers say what is the difference in cost of a keg of nails 112 lbs. weight in England, Germany, and America? The greatest feature in the cheap production of articless

in America is this. The American workman care nothing for methods, all he thinks of is the amount he receives on pay day. He will try anything and every- thing his employer puts to him, but he will have his share of the profit. All that Americans can do can be done by Englishmen if they will it to be so. In the letter of Mr. Stott, secretary of the Warring- ton Trades and Labour Council, I find very good advice given. With him I believe Protection will put off the day when labour the world over will recognise the fact that it must be united against the common enemy, the capitalist. A plasterer's labourer in America gets 14s. for eight hours with double that for over-time. Can Mr. Chamberlain work the sum out to show this an outcome of Protection? But I must conclude by notic. ing Mr. Stott a little more. I ask your workingmen readers to turn to his letter of June 20th and read over again the latter portion. Some of the readers of the "Examiner" will remember the series of articles I wrote some years ago on Mining Royalties. About the same time a gentleman of Wigan, I think, wrote a pam- phlet on the same subject. I wonder what amount of good we did? None, I'm afraid. I am led to this remark by the closing words of Mr. Stott. After a statement- of conditions and rem edies 'this is the kind of Protection we require. It can be done, shall it be done?" What is it that must be done? You must send men to Parliament who will not turn back the fingers of time on an outrageous Education Bill. We in America never have a "religious difficulty" making men into enemies and robbing the schools of the power to make real men and women. The men to represent the best interests of England are those who have studied the land question and all that it means. Will your prasent member any more than your late mem- ber ever touch the land question, even with a long pole? You know he will not, and yet you vote for him, and you deserve to suffer. Your member Should be able to tell you how the Patent Laws of America help the inventors and so help the worker. He should be able to follow, if he cannot lead, and then you would know what an American Industrial Engineer is, and so have no need to cry out for Protection. I differ from Mr. Stott only in one instance, I would not "control the trusts and syndicates!" let them severely alone. Wait, and before long you will see a heap of good come out of them. They are on the best lines of evolution, and only a bit awkward in learning how to walk.

advantage. Sir Robert makes a fatal mistake, and one which, if adopted and persevered in, would ruin the country. That, comparatively, we have but little to gain from the foreigner, I admit, but the vital question is not how much we can take from I him, but howlong, if we have the open door, will he I be, before he has the lion's share of the' colonial , trade, and deprive us of a large part of . what we now have. If this were allowed 1 to take place, it would ruin the whole 4ountry. There is the issue, which no one appears to see. Our ever-increasing. population necessitates that we must have increased trade to support it, but where is it to come from ? Seeing that for twenty years our breign exports have been stationary, we can have no hope there. If our trade with the Colonies and British possessions, as the statistical abstracts show, increased at the rate of two millions a year, we might safely count that, under preferential treat- ment, it would average at least three millions. This would give us, in fifteen years (includ- ing what we have now) 150 or 160 millions of safe and profitable trade. This would surely be worth some sacrifice, but this is for , the workers and the country to consider. It is impossible for our opponents to deny, that, if the door remains open, the foreigner will deprive us of a great portion of the trade that we now have, for if, as statistics show, we cannot compete in our home market, what possible chance shall we have in the colonial? For instance, take our lead- ing industry, textiles. In 1883 we imported 22 millions, and in 1902 it was 37 millions, or an in- crease of 15 millions. If such is the case in the home markets, what can we, after a time; expect in the colonial? It appears to me that it would be folly, not be say madness, to run the risk. *hat we want is a safe,prosperous, and progressive trade, even if we have to pay something more for our food.

all, man does not live by bread alone. There was something higher than a love of bread, and that was a love of country. He believed that the workingman, if he cheerfully paid, and submitted to pay two shillings extra on corn for the good of the Empire during the late war, he woald be quite ready to continue to pay it, and possibly more, if—and that was a very large if— it was mule quite clear to him that the exigencies of the united Empire required it. Whether they were Fair Traders or Free Traders he would say, examine for yourself the question. If it be wrong in principle, then a rigid examination would bring it to naught; but, if it be sound, then let them beware of ignorantly opposing it, lest haply they found themselves fighting against the well-being of the country. (Hear, hear.)' These guarded phrases, coming from a man of much travel and wide experience, should carry great weight with all who,hitherto,have refused to allow themselves to entertain what Mr. Chamberlain so pithily terms the "open mind."

ONE-SIDED FREE-TRADE v. PROTECTION.

Lord Masham's Criticism of the Board of Trade; and his , Analysis of Sir Robert Ciffen's Figures. Mr. H. P.Hughes writes : Sir,—ln my letter, published in your issue of 11th July, I mentioned that Lord Masham had very kindly given me permission to make use of any of his exceed- ingly valuable contributions to the Free-trade v. Pro- tection controversy. The two sets of quotations lam now giving, in this letter, are selected—not merely because they are recent and up-to-date, but rather because, in these extracts, Lord Masham, with his characteristic fearlessness, handles—with consummate -ease and lucidity—difficult and technical "points" which very few other public writers or speakers possess, in a sufficient degree, the powerful combination of knowledge and courage and social position to success- fully tackle. The name of Lord Masham's firm—the great firm of Listers of Bradford—is a household word, in Yorkshire. I am told that Mr. Lister rose—by sheerability—from a working man to be a real live lord, and, if true, all . honour to him for it. Of course, Mr. Chamberlain's • high position, and his empire-responsibility, constitute • him the greatest exponent of British Protectionist doctrines that ever lived; but, as a dogged, persistent, plodding, irrepressible, and competent champion of protectionist theories, Lord Mash= was a recognised public authority, Jong years before Mr. Chamberlain took the boldplunge which is already racking history. Several of my friends confront me with Board of Trade figures, as being opposed to my line of argument, and the words "Board of Trade" are spoken, astit were with bated breath, as if that authority was simply As a useful public service, let me now quote from a recent instructive article by Lord Masham. He says : "The Board of Trade during Lord Farrer's time, used; to delight the faithful [Free-traders], year after year, with a Free-trade balloon, showing how prosperous we were, and we have had much of the same thing ever since. The Yorkshire Post' pub- lished recently a summary of the last year's Board of Trade report. Perhaps the report (balloon) is not quite so gassy as it used to be, but it is very misleading. We are told that, in the 10 years 1890 to 1900, our exports have increased 6 per cent., but it is all a delusion, as our exports to foreign countries, of British manufactures, have not increased a shilling in twenty years. By putting foreign exports and the colonial together, there has been some increase, but this is not shown in the report ; to be worth anything, Ire should hare the foreign and the Colonial put separately." "Why do not the Board of Trade skew us, as they might easily do, the true state of our exports to foreign countries, instead of trying to hide it? And then let there be a com- parison made, between British Free-trade progress and the progress of Protectionist countries." A few days later than the publication of Lord Mas- ham's above-quoted criticism of the Board of Trade, I saw, in the "Daily Mail," a confirmatory passage, which I give without comment. It speaks for itself : "Board of Trade efficiency: Yesterday the pro- mised committee to inquire into the administration and efficiency of the Board of Trade and the Local Government Board was appointed by the Govern- ment. The following have agreed to. act :—Lord Jersey (chairman), Sir John Gorst, M.P., Mr. Alfred Emmott, M.P., Sir Charles Lister Ryan, late Comptroller and auditor-general, Sir* James Lyle • Mackay, R.C.I.E. (who negotiated a comraer cial treaty with the Emperor of China.) The appointment of the committee arose out of a conviction on the part of the business men of the House of Commons that the Board of Trade is I unequal to the commercial needs of the nation. The inquiry may result in the appointment of a Minister . . of Commerce." The Local Government -• Board has long been known as the "lumber room of the Government. Leaving the Board of Trade, let us now pass along to Lord Masham's views upon some of the figures that are served-up to us, by eminent authorities. Sir Robert Giffen is one of the greatest. living authorities upon what I would term National Statistics, and it takes a first-rate man to tackle him, upon figures. Well, Lord Masham is equal to the task, as will be seen from the following keen analysis : "Sir Robert Giffen, in a long article in the I 'Nineteenth Century,' quoted, from a statistical abstract, some really very remarkable figures, shew- I ing, very clearly, that, if we are losing ground in ; one direction, we are taining rapidly in another. ; He says that the statistical abstracts show that our exports to the Colonies and British possessions, in I 1887, were 75 millions, and that, in fifteen years I they had gone up to 105 millions (in 1901), and last year, 1902,they jumped up four millions more— only four millions! Consider this, in a single year It would probably be mainly owing to the war, and for that reason more or less fictitious. Taking the average of the fifteen years, there appears to have been an increase of about two millions a year, and yet, with this great fact before his eyes, he writes six pages to show that the colonial trade.is oo small, and there is so little to gain, that preferential trade would be of no
