THE ELESTION IN ST. JOHN'S WARD.

MR. THORNTON SECURES A LARGE

MAJORITY.

Few municipal • elections in Warrington have aroused such intense local interest as that which was decided in St. John's Ward on Thursday. Neither political party entered the fray and the main ques- tion before the electoi's was that of municipal trading. Curiously enough both candidates professed themselves to be non-traders,but Mr. Thornton had the advantage of being the nominee of the Non-trading; Committee with which he has been connected since its inaugura- tion. Mr. Magian, on the other hand, has never been connected with the committee, but he claimed to have been a Non-trader and to have fought the Corporation before the committee came into existence. Mr. Maginn expressed his views of the trading question as follows : Was it fair that any man—no matter who he might ba—who would undertake to:do the work honestly, straightforwardly and to the best of his ability,' should be debarred from his share of the trade of the town? (Applause.) He did not say that there should be one bit of favouritism shown to him. But they did not mean to say that When a man was a town councillor he was to debar himself from the ordinary trade he was entitled to? (Applause.) He was quite entitled to an equal share of the trade if his tender was lower than that of anyone else.

But I do not at all agree with Mr. Chamberlain that it will be necessary to increase the cost of living to such u point• as to increase wages, and I am very sure that they will not be increased by the higher cost of living, as wages, like everything else, are governed, and rise and fall, by the supply and demand. That wages would rise, is certain, as Protection will always give more employment. By a careful and proper readjustment of taxation, I see no reason why the workers' cost of living should be increased. Mr. Chamberlain's great Birmingham speech was the most courageous, but, at the same time, the most unwise, that he ever made, causing general and unnecessary alarm throughout the country. He would have been more %cautious about raising the food question if he had, as I did, some twenty years ago, in the Craven district, fought the big and little loaf stuck on two poles, with a drum and fife band, going from place to place vote-catching. On that occasion I was well beaten, and so would he probably be, if the General Election were to take place now. But the working classes are far, far away more educated than they were, and far more capable of grasping the situation. One thing is certain, that if they were so foolish as to miss the present oppor- tunity of securing, by preferential trade, what our loyal colonies offer us, they would be committing nothing less than national suicide." These two sets of quotations, from Lord Masham's recent article in " The Dispatch" will, I hope, be care- fully studied by all who are examining for themselves the statistical foundations upon which Free-Traders, and Protectionists base their opposite arguments Those who are taking an interest in this series of letters will—perhaps—have noticed that, as far as pos- sible, I am trying to avoid the ruts in which Protection and Free-trade discussions usually tend to run; and, although it is difficult to say anything that is really new, upon a subject that has been debated for over a century past, still, there is an opportunity for selecting the lesser-known or lesser-studied "points," and pre- senting them with—as it were—marginal up-to-date notes, and this is all I am attempting. Next week, I am planning to embody an outline or sketch, of Mr. Andrew Carnegie's views, written in 1898, upon "The Manchester Free-Trade School, and. To-day." He goes back to the times and theories of Villiers, Cobden, Bright, and their colleagues, and gives us a picture of what Free-trade was expected to do; what it actually did do; and where it fails to meet Britain's twentieth- century needs. With being written so long prior tothe Majority for Mr. Thornton ... 312 subject of Protection and Free-trade becoming a burn: the hreesßuiattrntio Officer Council (Aldermanmwhereer t t Sutton) voting raccli ing question, Mr. Carnegie's opinions are all the more taken place. • valuable and impartial. The Non-traders contended that no canaidate who held such views on the question could be a Non- trader. Mr. Thornton announced at the meeting on. Wednesday evening that he was "a thorough Non- trader to the backbone, and no efforts of his and no money of his would prevent him going the whole hog with the Non-Traders."

The election was vigorously fought. Mr. Magian threw himself heart and soul into the work, and with- out any further organization behind him he may be said to have fought the contest single-handed. He addressed four open air meetings and two under cover, and carefully ' canvassed the constituency. Mr. Thornton contented himself with two public meetings and received loyal support from the members of the Non-trading Committee. Mr. Thornton also received the assistance of the local members of the United Irish League, the President (Mr. C. Roddy) speaking at both meetings. A number of Irishmen, however, supported Mr. Maginn, but the splitting of the Irish vote told against him. Polling commenced in the Old Club Room, John- street at eight o'clock. The ward contains no fewer than 1,704 voters and excellent arrangements had been made for taking the poll by. the Town Clerk, who had appointed the following presiding officers :—Messrs. T. H. Spinks, J. R. Williams and J, H. Fisher. The poll clerks were Messrs. E. Lockett, A. Thorpe, and S. Bailey; Between 12 and two o'clock and from six o'clock to the closing of the poll, John-street presented a very animated appearance. The committee rooms were sur- rounded by the supporters of the respective candidates, and one after another, vehicles of all descriptions dashed up with the voters. Mr. Maginn had two large, motor-cars in use, and Councillor Dodds brought up one for Mr. Thornton. A workingmen's Committee worked hard to get the voters to the poll on behalf of Mr. Maginn, whilst many leading non-traders worked stren- uously on behalf of Mr. Thornton. Promptl3 at eight o'clock, the polling stations were closed and the ballot boxes removed to the Town Hall. The counting of the votes was commenced without delay, and before nine o'clock the result was announced to the public. It was found that 1234 electors had recorded their votes, being one of the heaviest polls on record for St. John's Ward. There were a number of spoiled papers. The result of the counting was as follows:

For Henry Thornton For John Maginn 458

think and vote for himself. (Cries "We have done it. ) He had been beaten, but would fight the wardagain and would win it. (Cheers and laughter.) Councillor PEMBERTON said he wished to thank them most heartily for the splendid fight they had made on behalf of the Non-trading party. If Mr. Maginn came out again he hoped he would come out in his proper colours—as a trader. Mr. Maginn had declared him- self as believing in the right of members of a Council trading with that Council, and the electors had shown that day that they were directly opposed to that.

MR. NORBURY WILLIAMS AT WARRINGTON. The Evidence of Mr. Henry Roberts at London.

A Challenge to Mr. Roberts.

Mr. Norbury Williams was the principal speaker at a meeting held in the Co-operative Hall, Winwick-street, on Wednesday evening, in support of the candidature of Mr. Henry Thornton. Councillor James Dodds presided, and there were also present on the platform :—Mr. H. Thornton (the candidate), Councillors W. J. Forshaw, John Pemberton, and A. Horrocks, Messrs. C. Roddy, A. Downes,C. Carruthers,A. Hulme,William Hickman, J. E Richardson, S. Gough, and others. ,',•4 _

Mr. THOMAS PEM-BiRTON controverted the statement of Mr. Maginn that the electric fighting station had cost £200,000. It had not cost one quarter that amount. Mr. Magma said he Rad been trying to find excuses for the action of Mr. Deas, but, he (Mr. Pemberton) con- tended it was not their duty to make excusesfor officials. He claimed on behallofithe Non-Traders that they had been frankly straight forward, and the figures they had given had not been contradicted. He reminded the electors that they were fighting for a principle. He was afraid those present had no idea of the want of backbone of some of the men in the Council. He did not think there were any workingmen in England who had stuck to a cause like the workingmen of Warring- ton had done to the Non-trading cause. (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN observed that he was proud of the way Warrington had stuck to the Non-trading cause. Mr. FORSHA.W referred to a statement by Mr. Magma that he (Councillor Forshaw) promised him the support of the Non-trading Association next November—if he would retire from the present contest in favour of Mr. Thornton. That statement was absolutely untrue. He (Councillor Forshaw) was not a member of the Non- trading Association,and that being:so, he could not, and I did not promise Mr. Magian the support of the Associa- tion next November.

Mr. CIIA.RLES RODDY said he was working with all his might for the return of Mr. Thornton. Instead of trying to cement a good feeling between the English and Irish people, Mr. Maginn was doing the opposite. A telegram was read from Councillor Lord saying be would give a whole day on the following day, and hoping others would do the same. Mr. Samuel Dunbobbin wrote :—" I cannot allow this meeting to close without wishing- Mr. Thornton every success. My reason for wishing it,is because in 1884 there was a strike with the wiredrawers at White- cross and Rylands Bros., which threw the labourers out of employment at both firms, numbering 150 men and boys for over 10 weeks. Mr. Thornton while the strike lasted, week in and week out, distributed loaves of bread and potatoes amongst the families of these men, and likewise gave me £25 to distribute Such noble actions as these should not be forgotten. Hoping the voters in St. John's Ward will rally round him and send him to the Town Council with a good majority." Mr. THORNTON, who had a hearty reception, asked his hearers whether they were satisfied with what the Town i Council had done n recent years. (Cries of" No.") He (Mr. Thornton) was a Non-trader to the backbone, and no efforts of his andno money of his, would prevent him from going the whole hog with the Non-traders. By doing so he felt he would be helping the working people of Warrington. (Hear, hear.; Mr. Thornton proceeded to show how the raising of assessments and rates had caused a rise in rents in Warri.ngtori._

SPEECH BY MR. NORBURY WIfLIAMS.

Mr. NORBURY WILLrAms referred to the fact that that was the 13th meeting he had addressed in Warring- ton. On the last occasion he was in another part of the town supporting the candidature of Mr. Horrocks. (Applause.) He was more than satisfied with the re- sult. It was a credit to the town; a credit to Mr. Dodds and his friends, and a credit to the cause of purity and good government. (Hear, hear.) Referring to Mr. Thornton's candidature, Mr. Williams said Mr. Thornton had been!a. Non-trader from the first. If it had been a case of a death-bed repentance he would not have said a word for him. (Laughter.) He hopedthat by returning Mr. Thornton, the Non-traders would secure one more of these constantly accumulat- ing victories which would make Warrington a name throughout England. These elections were being watched the country over by officials who were always ready to assume an authority which was not their by right. Warrington was doing more in this cause at present than any other borough he knew of in this country. Therefore this contest in .St. John's Ward was of greater importance than those who were in the midst of it might be inclined to think. By next November but one they hoped to have

===== 0002601_19030801_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
SWEPT THE TOWN THOROUGHLY CLEAN.

He believed Mr. Maginn was being supported by some big wigs of the town so as to knock one of the non- trading candidates down. Mr. Maeinn professed to be a Non-Trader,but his statement that no member of the Council ought to be debarred from his share of the trade of the town was entirely contrary to the position taken up by the Non-Traders. There was a clear line drawn between the two. Mr. Maginn had misapprehended the seriousness of the position. If anything, public service ought to mean loss and sacrifice and he (Mr. Williams) would look with great suspicion on the public services of any man who was not the loser in some way or other. He (Mr. Williams) would be sorry to think he was a farthing the gainer by anything he had done for the public. He knew he was thousands of pounds the loser, and therefore he deprecated with all his soul and being the thought of Mr. Maginifs, that a man on entering the Council might commence doing what he called his proper share of the trading. That was the seat and origin of the whole of this scandal in Warrington. It was the men who thought like Mr. Maginn who had ruined the fair name of Warrington. If Mr. Maginn had not uttered another seatence,the one referred to, ought to condemn him on Thursday. He (Mr. Williams) hadnot been present to hear any of the results,but he thought he would be present: on Thursday. (Hear, hear.) That sentence of Mr. Maginn's had worked him up. (Laughter). It was a scandal to Warrington that after two years of work such as the Non-traders had had, a candidate should so disgrace himself as to advocate that the old, bad, wretched system of trading should go on. If the election went against Mr. Henry Thornton it would be a greater disgrace than anything that had gone before. Did he not tell them two years ago that next to Blackpool, Warrington was the most corrupt borough in the kingdom? He did not say so to-night. Finer support had never been given to a municipal party than had been given to Mr. Dodds and his friends, and what was said two years ago, thank God had no need to be said to-night. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19030801_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON WAS REDEEMING. ITSELF,

but it did it by stages. It had a further opportunity now of doing something for the honour of Warrington and the redemption of its good name. (Applause.) Since he was last in Warrington the Town Clerk and Mr. Henry Roberts had been before the committee which had been . INQUIRING INTO MUNICIPAL TRADING.

This was a matter of more than local concern. Warring- ton had been the head and front of the trading, the im- moral trading between its Corporation and limiteLl com- panies with which members were connected. There had not been another borough in the Kingdom that had been worse than Warrington in this respect. He defied any man, including Mr. Roberts, to nameany single borough or city which had been deeper in the mire than War- rington had been. Mr. Roberts appeared before the com- mittee in London to give evideace in contradiction to what he (Mr. Williams) had slid. But when one gave evidence before a committee of seven lords and six commoners it was their practice, he found, to suddenly ask questions of you that you never dreamt you were going to be asked. (Laughter.) It was so with him (Mr. Williams) and it was so with Mr. Henry Roberts. He would say nothing about himself but he would say with reference to the evidence of Mr. Roberts that he was delighted with

lby Mr. Powell Williams, M.P., of Birmingham. It was one of the most interesting passages that he had ever read in municipal history. The people of Warrington knew Mr. Roberts at home amongst themselves; they knew the company he was connected with; they knew Ithe allegations that had been made against him with respect to that company and its trading with the Cor- poration whilst he was a member of the Council. Mr. Roberts did his best, he was bound to say, to exculpate his own company and himself in this particular matter, but when it came to the cross-questioning he (Mr. Williams) ventured to say that Mr. Roberts made such an exhibition as he (Mr. Williams) would be ashamed to ka.ve had to make. He made the frank confession that the average trading of his firm with the Corporation for the last 14 years was not more than ‘£2,000 a year. THE POOHBAH OF WARRINGTON.

(Renewed laughter.) When he is the Mayor of Warring- ton he puts on the lowest price he can screw them down to, but when dealing as managing direc- tor of the Slate Company he gets the highest price that can possibly be obtained. (Laughter.) John Maginn was following in his steps and each had got a wrong ticonception of public duty. (Hear, hear.) There was a good deal that was not told- before the Committee in London. More was left untold than was actually told. He sometimes wished he had been there at the time. (Laughter.) The very basis of this Non-trading agitation was that no member of the' Council should trade with the Corporation directly or indirectly, either as contractors or sub-contractors. He, wanted to make a CHALLENGE TO MR. ROBERTS before that meeting. He had told that distinguished - Committee in London that the trading of his Company did not exceed £2,000 a year. He (Mr. Williams)' publicly challenged him that he should permit him (Mr Williams) to look into the books of the Slate Company with a view of ascertaining the volume of trade done with the Corporation during the last seven years,whether as contractor or sub-contractor. (Hear, hear.) He would venture to prophesy that the amount that would. be arrived at would not be the small figure which he had laid before the committee, but, would amount actu- ally to tens of thousands of pounds. (Shame.) He (Mr. Williams) wanted therefore that he or somebody else should be permitted to examine the books of the company so that they might know whether .£2,000 was all the company had profited or whether it was tens of thousands. He (Mr. Williams) gave him that challenge and he awaited his reply. It was his (Mr. Williams's intention to have given the committee. the' names of nine companies in Warrington which had traded with the Ccrpration but he was: cut off with the first company. Some day he intended that the members of the House should know that it was not the member of any one companY who had been trading with the Corporation,but the members of nine companies. Although Mr. Roberts had been for years a party to the s3lection of elective auditors by the two respective political parties, he went before the committee and said he was dead against that system; it was a bad system, it had gone on for years,and ought to be abolished.
